Both rules leave the same 3 trades — the Brent/copper weekly-vs-monthly pairs, 6 entries of which cash funds 3. Every candidate closed either at the identical instant or at least 1.17 h (4,217 s) apart: 245 pairs at a 0 s gap, 243 of which settled the same way (including 226/226 commodity and 15/15 financial; the other 2 are the Black Phone 2 anomaly below), and 1,661 pairs at least 1.17 h apart, ~60.7% of which settled the same way — the nearest of those a men's/women's UMES at NCCU pair, which settled the same way, and the nearest that settled DIFFERENTLY 2.17 h apart. What the close rule refuses and the competition rule keeps — 102 `KXMLBGAME`/`KXMLBSTGAME` pairs (39% settled alike) and every unlabelled category (e.g. Mentions) — makes no trade difference here: of the 1,661 refused pairs only 196 were ever open together (183 at a Monday checkpoint), 0 of the 153 mention pairs and 4 of the 140 MLB-vs-spring-training pairs. **1 hour is the operator's choice (2026-09-25)**, and gives exactly the 1-minute and 15-minute result on this corpus; what it gives up is margin (see the residuals). The population is the backtest's: `event_title` is blank on ~95% of its eligible records.

**Where it holds on both paths: games and same-instant listings.** Live `close_time` is the SCHEDULED close — for a game, tip-off plus a fixed number of days, which keeps a doubleheader's two games hours apart — and the backtest's is the REALIZED close. Checked on archived payloads: WIU at EIU M/W 2.5 h apart scheduled (01:30 vs 23:00) and 2.7 h realized; TN Tech at TN St. M/W 2.5 h scheduled; UCL/La Liga days apart on both; Brent weekly/monthly both 21:00Z, identical on both.

**`find_time_series_pairs` is deliberately NOT gated.** Since DR-67 identical wording cannot form a time-series pair (it states the same deadline spans, or none), so a same-title-only gate cannot relabel the trade — the concern that put the one-series rule on BOTH finders (see the dedup note under Key Patterns). A close gate there would also refuse every genuine cross-event deadline pair, whose closes differ by design. The relabel guard is pinned on both paths, undated ("Q") and dated ("by Dec 31, 2026"): `tests/test_scanner.py::TestDeadlineGuardFinders::test_identical_wording_closing_apart_forms_no_pair_of_either_type` (no pair from either finder, nothing out of `main._dedup_pairs`) and `tests/test_backtester.py::TestRunBacktestCrossTypeDedup::test_identical_wording_closing_apart_forms_no_pair_of_either_type` / `::test_nothing_reaches_the_dedup_when_the_closes_differ`.

**Observability.** `find_same_title_pairs` logs one silent-at-zero INFO line, `Same-title candidates refused because the two markets close more than 60 minutes apart (two different games or instants, not one question listed twice): N`, with its own prefix and neither "one event series" nor "same event ticker", which other tests pin as ABSENT. `_extract_pairs` logs that line VERBATIM (pinned by `TestSameTitleCloseGapBacktest::test_the_close_gap_line_is_the_live_finders_verbatim`, on a fixture of two non-combo series that reaches the gate) plus a BACKTEST-ONLY `Same-title candidate pairs refused because a market's close_time cannot be read (fail closed; the live finder drops such markets before grouping): N`. The split exists because the two paths do not see the same population: the cache's closes are all aware (9,156,436 of 9,156,436 end in Z), but a same-title group is not close-filtered — `_can_ever_enter` keeps a market whose close it cannot parse — while the live finder drops a missing `close_time` before grouping. **The two close-gap lines are verbatim twins for READABLE closes only.** A pair whose closes cannot be COMPARED — one naive beside one aware — is refused on both paths, but lands on different lines: the backtest counts it on its unreadable-close line (`_comparable_closes_dicts` returns `None`), while the live finder, whose gate has ONE line, counts it on the close-gap line, whose wording ("close more than 60 minutes apart") is then not literally true of it. That is unreachable today — every Kalshi close string ends in `Z`, so `_market_from_dict` parses them all aware, and every cached close does too — and it fails closed either way; it would first show up on a payload drift that mixed the two formats. `TestOneSeriesAndSameEventCounts::test_every_candidate_is_returned_or_counted_exactly_once` sums both new counts into its same-title identity. `main._no_pairs_msg` names the condition ("same-title: ≥5% price diff on two different series closing within 60 minutes", the bound read from the constant and rendered by `scanner.close_gap_bound_text`). With the same-title branch now refusing an undatable pair, the pipeline no longer hands `_fetch_candles_parallel` a market without a readable close, so BS-07's branch there is defence in depth, kept covered by a direct test (`TestRunBacktestMalformedTimestamps::test_the_candle_fetch_still_resolves_an_unparseable_close_to_nothing`).

**Not changed:** `main._dedup_pairs` and the pairs table, `reporter` and its pinned columns, `trader`, `historical` (no new data), `dashboard` and its goldens, `backtest.py`, `_can_ever_enter`, `SETTLED_PREFILTER_CACHE_TAG` and the assembled caches — the rule acts at PAIR time, so every cache stays valid (the DR-02 precedent). **Test fixtures moved with the rule** — the exact gate broke 18 tests, each fixed rather than loosened: `test_dated_identical_wording_is_same_title_only` (`tests/test_scanner.py::TestDeadlineGuardFinders` and `tests/test_backtester.py::TestRunBacktestCrossTypeDedup`) keeps its name and now holds an ALIGNED fixture closing at one instant — its positive control `mB3` shares that instant too, so the control still differs from the refused case in WORDING alone — while the differing-close expectation, NO pair of either type, moved to the new `test_identical_wording_closing_apart_forms_no_pair_of_either_type` on both paths (the relabel guard above), and `TestRunBacktestCrossTypeDedup` keeps `_MARKETS` as its differing-close fixture beside a new `_ALIGNED_MARKETS`; `TestTimeSeriesTieredThreshold::test_same_title_pairs_ignore_deadline_gap`, which pinned that a pair closing 20 days apart "is still a candidate", was inverted deliberately (now `test_same_title_pairs_ignore_the_deadline_tier_but_not_the_close_gap`); `TestActiveTickerRelease` was redesigned (TY now closes with TX and carries a second-Monday candle at 0.60, because aligning its close alone brought TY/TZ inside the 30-day cap); `_p5_corpus` gives each same-title family's two legs one close, the later of the two draws, with its rng draws unchanged; `TestOneSeriesAndSameEventCounts` gained the two prefixes and its `_fuzz` copies its source's close onto every other re-listed duplicate (keyed on the index, no extra draw); a few same-title fixtures (`TestExtractPairsCanonHandling`, `TestOldCacheToleranceMissingTickAndSubtitleFields::test_extract_pairs_same_title_handles_missing_fields`) gained a shared close locally — never in the shared `_md`; and SS-1's `_ss1_corpus` (read by `TestGroupableSubset`, `TestPrepareCandidatesOverASettledCorpus` and `TestPrefilterLinesSayItRanDuringAssembly`) broke no test but silently lost almost all of its same-title coverage — its fill's 50-day random close spread left 4/5/4 same-title pairs for seeds 0/1/2, against 126/117/93 before the gate — so the fill records whose titles can only ever pair as same-title (`_SS1_SAME_TITLE_ONLY_TITLES`) now close at one shared instant, restoring 78/65/50 while the Rain/Snow fill keeps its random closes (so the gate's refusals stay covered too), the rng stream and every time-series pair unchanged, and `TestGroupableSubset::test_prepare_candidates_equals_grouping_the_whole_eligible_list` now asserts at least 40 same-title pairs so that coverage cannot silently drain again.

**Known residuals of DR-74.** Recorded here, not fixed.
- **Placeholder closes (live only).** Futures that can be decided early carry a family-wide PLACEHOLDER as their live `close_time` — all 30 live `KXOSCAR*` events close at 2027-12-31T15:00Z, and 20 of 34 `KXEARNINGSMENTION*` events at 2027-03-31T14:00Z — so two such listings pass live at a 0 s gap while the backtest separates them by realized close. The one cross-series identical-wording pair on the 2026-09-23 live snapshot is an example: `KXOSCARVIS-27-TIE` vs `KXOSCARMAH-27-TIE`, where the Visual Effects event carries the Makeup event's wording, so these are two different questions and only the price filter stops them today. The competition rule would not catch this class either (no label). The backtest's version of the class: 89 co-open non-game pairs refused only on realized close, 71 of which settled the same way (e.g. `KXNCAAFPLAYOFF` vs `KXNCAAPLAYOFF`: 54 pairs, 43 alike). Not a regression — these pairs were admitted before DR-74 too. An admitted cross-series pair is at most 3,600 s apart by construction, so no dry run can reveal a placeholder close; review each admitted cross-series pair by hand for a family-wide shared close.
- **Simultaneous or close tip-offs (live).** A M/W pair whose scheduled closes fall within an hour of each other passes live: two games at the same time (neutral-site tournaments), or back-to-back games less than an hour apart. The M/W pairs checked were 2.5 h apart.
- **1-hour margin (backtest).** The nearest refused candidate on the 365-day corpus closed 1.17 h from its partner (UMES at NCCU, M/W), ~10 minutes beyond the bound, so a M/W pair whose games END within an hour of each other would be admitted there. None did on this corpus. A 15-minute bound would have left 1 h of margin at no cost in trades; the operator chose 1 hour.
- **Realized-close drift (backtest only).** 4 duplicate listings, `KXTOPSONGTOTALGOL-2..5` vs `KXTOPSONGTOTALGOLDEN-2..5`, are refused because their realized closes drifted 1.5 h apart (all settled "no"; none entered). The album-vs-song-chart pairs are rightly refused (their sibling family settled differently on 6 of 13).
- **Settlement anomaly.** `KXRTBLACKPHONE2` vs `KXBLACKPHONE2` have identical rules and close, yet one settled two months later with the opposite result (2 pairs). No rule can see it.
- **Missed trades.** A genuine listing on two series with different scheduled closes is refused (fail closed) — a missed trade, never a false one.
- **Promotion.** Both finders keep ONE best pair per group, so refusing a group's best candidate PROMOTES its runner-up (pinned by `TestSameTitleCloseGap::test_refusing_the_widest_candidate_promotes_the_runner_up`), and a pairs-table diff is not a pure deletion.
- **Baselines.** Pre-DR-74 same-title baselines (21 trades / +4.7% on the 365-day run) are not comparable.

**`subtitle` is not dependable on a market payload — ingest normalizes `yes_sub_title` into it (2026-08 drift).** Live-verified 2026-08-14: the live `/markets` endpoint, `/events?with_nested_markets=true`, and the signed `/historical/markets` archive ALL served payloads with no `subtitle` key; on 2026-09-15 a verification read observed payloads carrying `subtitle` AND `yes_sub_title`, so the fallback is defence against a field that comes and goes, not the only source. The distinguishing outcome label arrives as `yes_sub_title` (e.g. the candidate names under one shared `title` of "Who will the next Pope be?"), with `no_sub_title` carrying the negated phrasing. This is not cosmetic: `subtitle` is the only intra-title discriminator in BOTH grouping keys — the same-title key `(event_title, title, subtitle)` used by `scanner.find_same_title_pairs()` and `backtester._group_by_exact_title()`, and (since DR-01) the time-series key built by `scanner.time_series_group_key()` for `scanner.find_time_series_pairs()` and `backtester._group_by_normalized_title()`. With every market parsing to `subtitle=""` the key collapsed to `(event_title, title)`, so two **different** outcomes sharing one question title on different event tickers grouped as the *same* contract and were traded under the 95% co-resolution assumption — a real-money false positive (reproduced offline: two distinct papal candidates paired with `tradeable=True`). The fix is at ingest only: `scanner._market_from_dict()` and `historical._market_to_dict()` read `subtitle` with a fallback to `yes_sub_title`, so the `ApiMarket.subtitle` attribute and the `"subtitle"` cache key keep their existing names and shape and no downstream code changed. Use `yes_sub_title`, never `no_sub_title` — the negated phrasing would make the grouping key asymmetric between the YES and NO framings of one outcome. Display fallbacks (`scanner.market_title`, `backtester._pair_key`, the backtester's trade labels) read the now-populated subtitle and silently improve: a title-less market falls back to its outcome label instead of the opaque ticker. **Cache caveat:** backtest day slices written before this fix carry `subtitle=None` and therefore reproduce the pre-fix weakened grouping — this is backtest fidelity only, with no live-money exposure — and `--no-cache` does NOT refresh them (day slices are deliberately reused unconditionally, see the sharded-fetch gotcha). A genuine refresh requires deleting `backtest_cache/archive_days/` and `backtest_cache/live_days/` **and** re-running with `--no-cache` (equivalently, also deleting the assembled `backtest_cache/settled_markets_*` cache files — the streamed `.jsonl.gz` and any legacy `.json`, since a legacy file is served whenever no `.jsonl.gz` exists at all) — on a default run the assembled per-`start_date` cache is loaded first and returns before the day slices are ever consulted, so deleting the slices alone changes nothing.

**Backtest fetch uses direct signed GETs — the pinned SDK has NO historical_api module.** `historical.build_historical_client()` returns a plain prod `KalshiClient`; `/historical/cutoff`, `/historical/markets`, `/historical/markets/{ticker}/candlesticks`, the live API's `/series/{series}/markets/{ticker}/candlesticks` (a market settled after the archive cutoff — see the archive-cutoff gotcha), and `/events/{ticker}` are reached via `historical._signed_raw_get()` (KalshiAuth signs timestamp+method+path, the SDK's rest client executes) composed with `_http.fetch_json_page()` for status checks and `api_call_with_retry` for backoff. Hard-won facts about the archive endpoint (all live-verified 2026-07-13): (1) it IGNORES every server-side time filter (`min_settled_ts`, `max_settled_ts`, etc. all return the identical page); (2) its page size is hard-capped at 1000 (larger → HTTP 400); (3) it is ordered by **`created_time` DESC, ticker DESC — NOT by settlement time** (settlements interleave hours out of order, and long-lived markets created before a date can settle long after it); (4) its pagination cursor is a urlsafe-base64 **protobuf of the keyset position** (field 1 = Timestamp of `created_time`, field 2 = ticker), which `historical._encode_archive_cursor()` reproduces byte-for-byte — this is what makes sharded parallel fetching possible. The live `/markets` endpoint honors `min_settled_ts` AND `max_settled_ts` server-side (page size also capped at 1000), but does NOT serve pre-cutoff settlements — once markets migrate into the archive they disappear from `/markets` entirely. Candlestick candles now arrive with the dollar string in `yes_ask.close` (the old `close_dollars` field is gone); the parser prefers `close_dollars` when present so both formats read as dollars, never cents.

**`fetch_all_settled_markets()` is sharded, parallel, and incrementally cached — don't serialize it again.** A sequential walk of the settled-market history is tens of millions of records at 1000/page (live-measured 2026-07-13: 95+ minutes for 8,300 pages without finishing). The fetch therefore: (a) runtime-verifies archive cursor synthesis (`_archive_cursor_synthesis_ok` re-encodes page 1's last record and byte-compares against the server cursor); (b) fetches one slice per UTC **created-day** via synthesized cursors across `SETTLED_FETCH_MAX_WORKERS` (config.py, 8) threads, persisting each completed slice to `backtest_cache/archive_days/<day>.json.gz`; (c) starts those created-days at `ARCHIVE_FIRST_CREATED_DATE` (config.py, 2021-06-01, a month below the archive's oldest market; or at `start_date`, if a window starts earlier still), NOT at `start_date` — the archive is created-ordered and has no time filter (`/historical/markets` filters only by `tickers`, `event_ticker`, `series_ticker` and `mve_filter`), so a market created before start_date that settles in-window can be found only by reading its created-day, and the assembly's `settle >= start_ts` test drops the earlier days' other records. A sequential **tail** walk below `created_time == start_date` did this job until 2026-10-01 and stopped after `ARCHIVE_MAX_BARREN_PAGES` empty pages; on a 2025-10-01 start it stopped at page 2,813 and missed 60 in-window markets created in 2023-2024 (`LEAVEPOWELL-25-DEC31`, `GTA6-25DEC31`, …) behind runs of 56 and 59 empty pages. The days before the window are small (for a 2025-10-01 start, 1,583 days = 3,001 pages = 2.97M markets, about 58 MB of slices; the day workers fetched those 1,583 slices in 2 min 53 s on 2026-10-01, against the tail's 11 minutes), are reused like every archive slice while the cutoff holds, and are checked once per run: `_check_archive_floor` asks for the newest market created before the first day and WARNs if there is one. An assembled `settled_markets_*` cache built before this change lacks those markets and is still served on a hit — rebuild it with `--no-cache`; (d) sweeps the post-cutoff range as parallel per-**settled-day** windows via `min/max_settled_ts`, persisting fully-elapsed days to `backtest_cache/live_days/`. Any runtime-assumption failure raises `_ShardedFetchUnsupported` and falls back to the original sequential walks (which rely only on documented behavior) — never delete the fallbacks or the runtime checks. **The whole phase is skipped when `start_ts >= cutoff_ts`** (an INFO line says so): the archive holds only pre-cutoff settlements, so no record can satisfy such a window, and proving that by running the cursor-synthesis probe and the walk below start_date cost ~18 seconds and ~50,000 parsed records on every post-cutoff run. Note the skip also silences the synthesis probe on that path, so such a run reports on cursor synthesis at all (TS-25). **The `_fetch_archive_sequential` fallback stops on a barren-page counter, not on a settlement comparison.** No exact stop rule exists on a created-ordered walk: a market created arbitrarily early can settle in-window, so no page proves deeper pages are unproductive. The archive walks used to stop at the first page whose `page[0]` (the newest-*created* record on it, whose settlement time says nothing about the rest of the page) settled before `start_ts` — since most Kalshi markets are short-lived, that fired after one or two pages and silently dropped exactly the long-lived in-window settlers (BS-02). They now stop after `ARCHIVE_MAX_BARREN_PAGES` (config.py, 50) **consecutive** pages containing zero in-window settlements — the same productivity bail-out idiom as `EVENT_TITLE_LISTING_MAX_BARREN_PAGES` / `MVE_MAX_EMPTY_PAGES`, ~50k records of created-time depth past the last productive page. That rule is still not complete — it is what missed the 60 markets above — so the sharded path does not use it and reads every created-day instead; the fallback keeps it because a one-page-at-a-time walk of the whole archive would take hours, and it runs only when cursor synthesis fails. Productivity is judged on ANY in-window settlement on the page, deliberately **independent** of the binary-result and created-time keep filters, so a run of all-voided pages can't spuriously trip the counter. Don't reintroduce a `page[0]`-based stop, and don't judge productivity on "records kept". The test oracle used to replay the same flawed rule, so the parity tests structurally could not catch this; it is now `_expected_in_window`, a direct predicate over the fixture records (see `tests/test_historical.py`). The cursor-synthesis probe issues a real request, so **any** unexpected exception from it (auth, outage, unparseable body) is wrapped into `_ShardedFetchUnsupported` too — it used to escape the phase and kill the run with no warning logged. Both pool loops call `pool.shutdown(wait=False, cancel_futures=True)` when a worker raises: the executor's `__exit__` would otherwise drain every still-queued day (hundreds of them — hours) before the fallback is even reached. **Progress-line labels are load-bearing for diagnosis:** the sharded and sequential paths emit the same line shape, so they are suffixed `[sharded]`/`[windowed]` vs `[sequential]` — with identical labels (the pre-2026-08-03 state) a log cannot tell you which path a run actually took, and a live run was misdiagnosed exactly that way. Each completed slice also logs `N/M complete` with a rate and ETA; don't drop it, it's the only signal of how far a multi-hour fetch has actually got. Slice-reuse rules are load-bearing: archive day files are stamped with the `cutoff_ts` they were fetched under and are ONLY reused while it matches the current cutoff (a cutoff advance migrates markets archive-ward and OFF the live endpoint, so stale slices would silently miss them); live day files cover immutable fully-elapsed UTC days and are reused unconditionally; the frontier (current) day is always refetched and never persisted. Day slices are deliberately reused even under `--no-cache` (they cannot go stale by construction) — that's what makes refresh runs cheap, and an interrupted fetch resumes at day granularity. **Slice records are never accumulated in memory — not across days, and not within one day.** Each pool worker (`_fetch_and_store_archive_day` / `_fetch_and_store_live_window`) persists its own slice and returns only a count, and each phase then hands back a LAZY `_DaySliceStream` from `_assemble_day_slices()` — nothing is read at that point, and every later walk re-reads the slices off disk one record at a time, newest day first, which is the ordering the caller's first-wins ticker dedup depends on (the live phase returns a `_RecordChain` of its frontier spool followed by its stream, the old `frontier + [...]` order). Don't "simplify" by having workers return their records and flattening them: that is exactly what OOM'd a full-history run (2.7 GB RSS only 17% through, growing superlinearly with GC pressure), and it also drags gzip/JSON serialization back onto the main thread where it can't overlap. **The ASSEMBLED corpus is never accumulated either (SS-1 Commit C, 2026-09-24).** A 7-day window's past days held 18,061,549 fetched records of which 7,260,952 passed the backtester's prefilter, at 3,926 B/record (~28 GB), and the old assembly held them in a ticker-keyed `selected` dict, then a list copy, then one whole-list `json.dumps` — the run reached 19.9 GB of physical footprint in that silent phase and thrashed until killed. One generator, `_assembled_records`, now reproduces the old `_merge` EXACTLY — sources in the old order (archive day slices newest-first — every created-day from the first, so the days before the window come after the window's own, where the tail's records used to — below `cutoff_ts`; then live: frontier, then live day slices newest-first, unbounded above), `settle >= start_ts`, the prefilter BEFORE the first-wins ticker dedup, a fresh `seen` set per walk — and `fetch_all_settled_markets` walks it TWICE: walk A (`_count_assembled`) counts what the `Historical endpoint`/`Live endpoint` lines report (the same kept numbers, the same line order — since M9 each line also carries the endpoint's settled records and what the prefilter and the dedup removed, see the prefilter gotcha) and collects the event tickers for `_load_or_build_event_titles` (unchanged); walk B patches `event_title` exactly as before (only when `titles` is truthy) and streams each record straight into the assembled cache through `_DayStreamWriter` (new `write_record`), which is returned as a `SettledCorpus`. Walk B must reproduce walk A — same count and the same order-sensitive `_assembly_identity` over ticker and event_ticker — or `SettledCorpusError` is raised and the writer aborts, publishing nothing (a slice rewritten or pruned between the walks would otherwise write titles resolved for different records). Only tickers are held by a walk, never a record; what stays resident is a sequential fallback's list (as before), the walk's ticker set, the event-ticker set and the title map — the last three scale with the corpus (per-ticker strings, not records). Measured on the 2026-09-24 7-day run (3,989,846 event tickers): the fresh fetch peaked at 2,782 MiB before grouping against 895 MiB for the cache-hit rerun of the same window, and the watchdog placed the fresh run's peak (2,957 MB footprint) at the event-title merge and save, not in either walk — the cost DR-51 removed (see the event-title gotcha). On a synthetic 4,000,000-ticker request, building the set took the process's peak RSS from 51 to 673 MiB, and a steady-state (post-DR-51) title call added 145 MiB on top — mostly the returned title map, one entry per requested ticker (a 4,000,000-entry dict filled one key at a time measures 117 MiB by sys.getsizeof). Pinned by `tests/test_historical.py::TestStreamedAssemblyParity` (the old merge kept verbatim as an oracle, on list and stream sources and on the real phases with each fallback) and `::TestNothingIsMaterialized` (weakref-tracked: zero disk-parsed records alive while titles are resolved, at most the record in hand plus the open file's meta line at every cache write and every corpus step). Don't turn the corpus, a phase's stream or the assembly back into a list anywhere in the pipeline (`list(...)`, indexing, `frontier + [...]`), and don't give a mid-walk read failure a fallback or a skip. A worker no longer holds a whole day either (BS-15): at 2026-08 volumes one UTC day is ~4.4–4.8M markets, and buffering a day per worker sawtoothed RSS 3.4 → 4.3 → 7.89 GB on a 16 GB host. `_fetch_archive_day` / `_fetch_live_window` therefore take an optional `emit` callback; with it set they flush and drop their buffer every `SETTLED_FETCH_CHUNK_RECORDS` (config.py, 50k — the real bound is that plus the page in flight, since flushes land on page boundaries) and return a COUNT instead of a list. No production caller passes `emit=None` any more (SS-1, fixed 2026-09-24): past-day workers stream into their slice file, and the frontier (never-persisted, partial) day streams through `historical._extend_kept`, a sink that applies the caller's `keep` predicate (the backtester's `_can_ever_enter` prefilter) to each batch as its pages arrive and writes the survivors into a `historical._FrontierSpool`, so no frontier record — rejected or kept — is resident beyond the one batch in flight. The frontier used to be accumulated UNFILTERED and filtered only after the whole pool drained — the same records in the same order, but at a peak that grows through the UTC day (1.9M records at 07:30 UTC on 2026-09-24; a full day is up to 9.2M) whatever the window length, and that alone peaked a 7-day backtest at ~5.5 GiB RSS. Don't restore the post-hoc filter. The sink runs on the frontier's worker thread (so `keep` must be thread-safe — `_can_ever_enter` is pure) and the main thread seals and reads the spool only after the frontier future's `result()`. `frontier_future.result()` is LOAD-BEARING: it is the only place a frontier-window failure (an exhausted retry, a non-transient error, `keep` raising on the worker, a spool write failing) surfaces, and the pool's exit waits for the worker either way, so deleting it returns a silently SHORT frontier instead of raising — pinned by `TestFrontierStreamsThroughKeep`'s failure tests. Its return value, discarded until M9, is how many records the frontier window emitted BEFORE `keep`, so the gap to the spool's length is exactly what the prefilter dropped there; that count joins the assembly's `AssemblyCounts` only once the windowed path has succeeded, so a frontier the live fallback discards is never counted beside the sequential walk that refetches its day. **Why a spool and not a list (SS-1 review, fixed 2026-09-24):** filtering as pages arrive still left the keep-passing frontier resident as a list, and its size depends on the WEEKDAY, not on the window's length — `_can_ever_enter` admits every market open over a Monday checkpoint on/after `start_date` that closes at least a day later, so a frontier captured the day after a Monday can be mostly eligible (7,190,452 of the 9,176,306 records settled on Tuesday 2026-09-22 passed `_can_ever_enter(m, 2026-09-17)`, ~28 GB at 3,926 B/record on a 16 GB host). The spool is an ANONYMOUS temporary file (`tempfile.TemporaryFile` in `CACHE_DIR`, unlinked on creation): no later run can find it or reuse it as a complete day, and the OS reclaims it on close or on any process death, so a crash cannot leave a stale one. It carries the day slices' line framing and no meta block, is walked once per assembly walk (a newer walk invalidates a suspended one, which raises if resumed, since they share one file position; a complete walk that ends on a different count raises `SettledCorpusError`), and is closed by `fetch_all_settled_markets` in a `finally` (via `_RecordChain.close()` / `_close_records`) once its two walks are done, on success and failure alike — so the phase's result cannot be walked after the fetch returns. Don't turn it back into a list. Pinned by `tests/test_historical.py::TestFrontierSpool` (no kept frontier record alive at any page request or after the phase; exact old order on every walk; anonymous; closed on success, on a failed assembly and on a failed phase; lifecycle misuse loud). The list-returning `emit=None` form is retained for direct callers; the sequential fallbacks (`_fetch_archive_sequential`/`_fetch_live_sequential`) are separate walks with no sink that apply the same `keep` per record as each page arrives — exact, because the merge re-applies the prefilter before its first-wins dedup, and their progress lines still count records before `keep`; since M9 each also adds what it dropped to the assembly's counts, since the assembly never sees those records — but still hold their whole keep-passing result; a live-phase fallback first closes the partial frontier spool (freeing its disk), since the sequential sweep refetches today anyway. **Two slice formats exist on disk and both must stay readable:** the legacy single gzipped JSON document `{"meta":…, "markets":[…]}` written by `_day_store_save` (hundreds of MB already cached; refetching them costs hours), and the streamed `"jsonl-v1"` format written by `_DayStreamWriter` — meta line first, then one compact market dict per line, in fetch order (server newest-settled-first, which is what preserves the newest-first assembly / first-wins dedup contract). Both writers are kept: the streaming one for the workers, `_day_store_save` for callers already holding a full list, so neither format rots. `_DayStreamWriter` writes to `<path>.tmp` and only `commit()` renames it into place, same atomicity contract as `_day_store_save` — a killed worker leaves no visible slice. `_day_store_iter` is the ONE reader of the format (since SS-1's Commit C; `_day_store_load` is its all-or-nothing list form, `_DaySliceStream` and `SettledCorpus` its streaming users) and routes on file CONTENT (a first line that parses to a dict *without* a `"markets"` key is JSONL), **never on whether a whole-file parse succeeds**: an empty JSONL day is a lone meta line that parses fine as a document, so a parse-success test would misclassify every empty day. It parses JSONL one line at a time and yields each record, applying the optional `keep` predicate per record when a caller passes one (the prescan passes `_discard_all`; since M9 the phases pass none to their day-slice streams — see the prefilter gotcha), so a multi-million-record day is never materialized; the meta block is checked before the first record is yielded, and a fault found later — a malformed line, a truncated or damaged stream, including a damaged deflate block (`zlib.error`, which is NOT an `OSError` and used to escape the prescan as a crash), a non-object record or meta block — raises `_SliceUnreadable` AT the fault, after the records before it were yielded. So `_day_store_load` stays all-or-nothing (the WHOLE slice fails, `None` → refetch), while a streaming walk treats it as fatal: a slice that goes bad after its phase verified or wrote it raises `SettledCorpusError` naming the day and the remedy (re-run; the prescan then refetches it) — deliberately NOT the sequential fallback the old eager assembly took, which would hold the whole range in memory and could not take back records already yielded, and never a silently short corpus. An exception out of `keep` itself is not a file fault and propagates on both formats (before Commit C, a `keep` that raised `OSError`, `EOFError` or `ValueError` — the classes the old `except` caught — read as "slice unreadable" on the JSONL path only; any other exception already propagated). The prescan deliberately loads-and-discards to test slice validity (it passes `_discard_all`, so a streamed slice is fully parsed but nothing is retained), so reused days are decoded three times — the prescan and both assembly walks — which is minutes against a multi-hour fetch, and the alternative (partial-JSON meta peeking) is far more fragile. **The backtester's candlestick fetch (`backtester._fetch_candles_parallel`) is parallel for the same reasons**, across `CANDLESTICK_FETCH_MAX_WORKERS` (config.py, 8) threads, and uses the identical `pool.shutdown(wait=False, cancel_futures=True)` tear-down. It is safe because candle cache paths are strictly **per ticker**, so two workers can never target one path — `_save_json_cache` writes atomically (tmp+replace, so a truncated file can never become visible), but its tmp name is derived from the destination, so a shared cache path would still collide between workers; never introduce a fetch whose cache path is shared across workers. Reads go through `_load_json_cache`, which treats a corrupt/truncated file as a cache MISS (WARNING + refetch) rather than raising out of a worker. Worker exceptions must **propagate** out of `run_backtest`: `fetch_candlesticks` already fail-softs network errors to `[]` internally, so anything that still raises is a real defect (e.g. a market that should have been prefiltered out — the run_backtest tests rely on exactly that KeyError surfacing) and must not be degraded into "this ticker has no prices".

**The old fetch's multi-minute stalls were client-side memory/GC, not API throttling.** The 2026-07-13 live run showed growing pauses (up to ~14 minutes between page-100 log lines, growing roughly linearly with markets held) with ZERO retry warnings logged and consistent ~0.1–0.3s server latency — the cause was accumulating millions of RAW market payloads (30+ fields plus nested `mve_selected_legs` lists) in one Python list. The rewritten fetch normalizes every record to the compact `_market_to_dict()` form at keep-time and streams day slices to disk. Don't "optimize" by deferring normalization or accumulating raw pages again. The same failure recurred on 2026-08-03 in compact form — the phase functions still held every completed slice in a `results` dict *and* flattened it into a second list — so slices are now dropped as soon as they're persisted and re-read at assembly (see the sharded-fetch gotcha above). If a future run stalls again, check RSS before suspecting the API. **The failure class then moved one phase later, into the grouping/pairing step (TS-07).** `_prepare_candidates` (the first half of both `_prepare_entries` and `run_backtest_sweep`) held the whole eligible window as ONE list and built two group maps and two candidate-pair lists over it — until SS-1 (below), which stopped materializing the records that cannot group. Two measurements exist, both on a 16 GB host and both of the same 1,089,165-record FIVE-DAY window (the cheapest run the tool supports) — and they attribute the cost to two DIFFERENT places, so never quote one as if it explained the other. (1) **2026-09-12, cache MISS:** the fetch assembled the streamed day slices into that list and the recorded peak for the run is **6.05 GiB RSS**; that run predates the `_log_rss()` lines, so its own log carries no RSS line to confirm it — the figure was observed outside the log. (2) **2026-09-13, cache HIT:** `fetch_all_settled_markets` returned at its `use_cache` early return (`Loaded 1089165 settled markets from cache`) and assembled NOTHING; the memory went into `historical._load_json_cache`, which does `json.loads(path.read_text())` over the 1.43 GB assembled `settled_markets_*.json` — logged `Peak RSS before grouping: 3816 MiB` and `Peak RSS after pair extraction: 3977 MiB`, i.e. grouping and pair extraction added only 161 MiB to the high-water mark on that path. **That cache-load cost was a real, separate finding** (`_load_json_cache` materializes the whole file as one `str` and then every record from it); **SS-1's Commit C added the streaming reader** — new assembled caches are `settled_markets_*.jsonl.gz`, validated and then walked record by record through `historical.SettledCorpus` — so only a hit on a LEGACY `settled_markets_*.json` still pays it, exactly as before (those files keep loading, whole, as a list). The window is instrumented rather than invisible: `backtester._log_rss()` logs peak RSS in MiB (normalizing `ru_maxrss`, which is BYTES on macOS and KILOBYTES on Linux) at `before grouping` and `after pair extraction`. The `before grouping` line is logged **before** the RAM-budget warning so the two read in causal order: the RSS line is what the fetch or cache load — and, since SS-1, the two passes over the corpus — has ALREADY cost, and the warning (fired when the GROUPABLE subset exceeds `BACKTEST_MARKETS_RAM_WARN`; it counted every eligible market before SS-1) names that subset's share of it and what grouping adds on top. Two rules on that warning: it must not claim to precede the spend — by the time it fires every record it counts is already resident, which is why it counts the groupable records (what stays resident from the warning onward: the corpus is released just before it) and not the eligible ones — and it must print **only the running process's own numbers**, never a figure from another run, because nothing will ever prompt a reader to refresh a measurement baked into a string emitted on every run; the historical figures above belong in `config.py`'s comment and in this file, and nowhere else. `BACKTEST_RECORD_BYTES_ESTIMATE` (2,700) is the PARSED footprint of one cached record — what the list of dicts costs in memory — not its size on disk and not a peak-RSS predictor, since peak also covers whatever transients are live alongside it (the decoded JSON string on the cache-hit path; the group maps and pair lists during pairing). Two measurements on the real 2026-09-07 assembled cache bracket it: ~3.0 KB/record allocated when 195,038 sampled records are decoded under `tracemalloc`, and no more than ~2.4 KB/record resident once that 1.43 GB decoded string is subtracted from the 3,816 MiB pre-grouping peak — so it stays a round estimate between the two. The corpus (`markets`) and the first pass's key index are `del`'d right after the second pass, before any group map exists, and `ts_groups`, `same_groups` and `groupable` in ONE statement as soon as the pair lists are built, before the candlestick pool spawns its threads: nothing below reads any of them, and dropping the group maps ALONE frees no record dicts at all, because `groupable` still references every one of them. That release lowers RESIDENCY across grouping, the candlestick fetch and the `_find_entry` sweep; it does **not** lower peak RSS, which is a high-water mark already reached by that point. All of it is advisory — nothing is capped or dropped. **Known residual of that re-pointing:** when the corpus is a list (since Commit C, only on a LEGACY `.json` cache hit), every eligible record WAS resident up to its release and the `before grouping` RSS line includes all of them, yet the warning does not count them — so a run whose eligible count is far above the threshold but whose groupable count is not (the 7-day window below: 7,260,952 eligible, 184,255 groupable) gets no warning for the list that set its peak; the eligible count is still on the `Markets to analyze` and `Groupable subset` lines, and the cost on the RSS line. **SS-1 (2026-09-24): only the groupable eligible records are grouped, and `_prepare_candidates` builds no eligible list of its own.** A 7-day window (`--start-date 2026-09-17`) found 7,260,952 of 18,061,549 fetched past-day records eligible (99.75% of them MVE combos) at a measured 3,926 B/record under `tracemalloc` — ~28 GB for the eligible list alone — yet both grouping functions drop every single-member group, and only 184,255 of those records share a time-series OR same-title key with another eligible record; the rest can never appear in any pair of either type. `_prepare_candidates` therefore walks the corpus TWICE, treating it as any re-iterable of market dicts. **The corpus itself stopped being a list in SS-1's Commit C.** Until then `fetch_all_settled_markets` returned ONE list, and because the prefilter is applied during its assembly that list IS the eligible set — resident through both walks until `del markets`, and on that window alone ~28 GB. It now returns a `historical.SettledCorpus` (see the sharded-fetch gotcha) that streams the assembled `settled_markets_*.jsonl.gz` cache on every walk, fresh dicts each time, and holds its record count as `len()`: a walk that cannot read the file raises `SettledCorpusError` (a `RuntimeError`), and so does a complete walk that yields a different count. The passes needed no change for it — they were written for any corpus that re-iterates identically — and `tests/test_backtester.py::TestPrepareCandidatesOverASettledCorpus` pins that a real streamed corpus prepares exactly what the list does, in exactly two walks. Only a hit on a LEGACY `.json` cache still hands over a list. What the commit before it removed is the second, filtered copy the old code built and the grouping over every eligible record. Pass 1, `_index_eligible_keys`, counts every record (the `Markets to analyze` figure), applies `_can_ever_enter`, feeds the outcome-label census (an `_OutcomeLabelTally`, so the census costs no pass of its own), and appends `hash()` of each eligible record's two keys to `array('q')` buffers with validity flags — about 27 B per eligible record while the walk runs (187 MiB at 7,260,952 eligible records, measured on synthetic keys, 2026-09-24), plus a transient of about 74 B per record (515 MiB at that count) while `_shared_key_mask`'s `np.unique` computes the keep flags: small beside the records it stands in for, but not "a few bytes" — through `_ts_group_key`/`_st_group_key`, the SAME single definitions `_group_by_normalized_title`/`_group_by_exact_title` now group on (AST-pinned in `tests/test_strategy.py::TestTimeSeriesKellyParity::test_ast_both_finders_group_through_the_shared_key_helper`, which is a two-link chain since this change). `_shared_key_mask` (numpy) flags a hash that occurs at least twice among VALID keys of its kind. **Hashing is safe in exactly the direction that matters:** equal keys always hash equal, so a shared key is always kept, and a 64-bit collision can only keep an EXTRA record, which the exact grouping then drops as the singleton it is — results never move (pinned by forcing every hash to collide). Pass 2, `_materialize_groupable`, re-applies `_can_ever_enter` and keeps the flagged records BY POSITION among eligible records — the normalize_title-dominated keys are never recomputed for the records it drops — and **raises `RuntimeError`** if the corpus did not re-iterate identically in anything the mask was chosen from: a different eligible count, or at any eligible position a different `_corpus_identity` — one hash over the ticker and every field either grouping key reads (`event_title`, `title`, `subtitle`) — so a same-count reorder, or a grouping field changed between walks under unchanged tickers, is refused rather than silently misaligning the mask. No other field is compared, because none can change which records are grouped; eligibility is re-applied by pass 2 itself. Every member of every group of two or more is kept, in its original relative order, so the group maps (keys, members, member order, insertion order), both pair lists, the census and every reported number are exactly those of grouping the whole eligible list; the census still logs from its old position through `_report_outcome_label_coverage`, after the RAM warning. Log lines are unchanged in text and relative order except one new INFO line after pass 2 (`Groupable subset: materializing %d of %d eligible markets — ...`) and the reworded RAM warning (`%d groupable markets (of %d eligible) are materialized for grouping: ...`). Not changed: `_can_ever_enter`, both grouping keys, pairing, Kelly, fees and `SETTLED_PREFILTER_CACHE_TAG`. The per-eligible-record cost is unchanged too — pass 1 computes each key once, exactly as the old full-list grouping did — so a long window's pass 1 is as silent as its old grouping step was. **Pruning each market dict to the keys the backtester actually reads is a specified but UNLANDED follow-up**, deliberately gated on a profiling measurement (accept only if peak RSS ≤ 3 GB and the `Potential pairs` counts are byte-identical on the same assembled cache); don't land a pruning diff without it.

**The live-sweep `min_settled_ts` must track `start_date`, not just `cutoff_ts` (fixed 2026-07-13).** `fetch_all_settled_markets()`'s live phase used to always pass `min_settled_ts=cutoff_ts` — correct for the common case (`start_date` far in the past, e.g. the default `2024-01-01`, where the archive already covers everything before `cutoff_ts` anyway), but catastrophic for a narrow recent `start_date`: live-verified 2026-07-13, requesting the last 7 days scanned 20k+ pages (20M+ records) over ~5 hours because the server was asked for everything since `cutoff_ts` (which can trail `now` by a long way) and the existing client-side `settle_epoch < start_ts` check just silently discarded almost every page. Since the live endpoint DOES honor `min_settled_ts` server-side, the fix is `min_settled_ts=max(cutoff_ts, start_ts)` — strictly narrows the server-side window (never widens it, so it can't skip markets the client-side filter wasn't already going to drop) and collapses a multi-hour scan to a bounded one for recent windows. Don't revert to a bare `cutoff_ts` — that reintroduces the unbounded scan for any caller that passes a recent `start_date` (e.g. a quick verification run or a future `--recent-only` mode). In the windowed live sweep this bound survives as the floor of the settled-day windows at the **phase** level: `live_min_ts = max(cutoff_ts, start_ts)` is computed once and never goes below that bound. The *per-window* `min_settled_ts` the server actually sees can be up to 86,399s below it, though — the first window rounds `live_min_ts` down to UTC midnight (`first_lo = live_min_ts - (live_min_ts % _DAY_SECONDS)`) so windows align on day boundaries. This is harmless: the live endpoint serves nothing before the archive cutoff regardless of what `min_settled_ts` asks for, and the client-side `_merge` step still drops any record with `settle < start_ts`. The existing tests use a midnight cutoff, so they cannot observe the rounding; don't read "tests assert the minimum" as proof the per-window value never dips below `max(cutoff_ts, start_ts)`.

**`KalshiClient` monkey-patch pattern:** The SDK does NOT accept `api_key_id` or `private_key_pem` as constructor parameters. They must be set as attributes on the `Configuration` object:
```python
cfg = Configuration(host=url)
cfg.api_key_id = key_id        # detected by hasattr() inside SDK
cfg.private_key_pem = pem_text
client = KalshiClient(configuration=cfg)
```
Do not "fix" this by moving them to the constructor — it will break silently.

**Fee ceiling rounding at small `n`:** `fee_leg_exact(1, 0.5)` = `ceil(0.07 * 1 * 0.5 * 0.5 * 100) / 100` = `ceil(1.75) / 100` = `$0.02`. At `n=1`, fees round up aggressively. The exact payoff check `min_payoff > 0` in `compute_trade()` (`min_payoff` is the profit floor for a same-title pair and the profit in either WIN cell for a time-series pair — the in-between cell loses `total_cost_with_fees`) catches this and returns `None` — this is intentional, not a bug. Also: `fee_leg_exact()` rounds to 6 decimals *before* the ceiling so binary float noise (e.g. `175.00000000000003`) can't bump an exact-cent fee up an extra cent — don't remove the `round()`.

**Ambiguity is resolved by position DELTA, never by absolute holdings.** An exception from `create_order` doesn't prove rejection (a timeout can land after the fill), so `_execute_one` consults the account — but the account's *absolute* position in a ticker is not evidence about a specific order: it may already hold contracts from an earlier run or a manual trade. `_execute_one` therefore reads `_position_count()` immediately **before** each submission (the NO leg's ticker first, then the YES leg's) and again after an ambiguous one, and attributes only `_fill_delta(before, after)` — expected `−no_leg.count` for the NO leg and `+yes_leg.count` for the YES leg. The old absolute checks were wrong in both directions: a stale external YES holding made an unfilled YES leg report `"executed"` (leaving the NO leg unhedged while the log claimed a complete pair), and a stale external NO holding made an unfilled NO leg trigger a `reduce_only` sell that liquidated an unrelated position. Compare deltas with `trader._DELTA_EPS` (`1e-6`) — fills are whole contracts, but `position_fp` arrives as a string and is float-parsed. Never reintroduce a bare `held == 0` / `if held:` test. **A delta of ZERO is not, by itself, a confirmed non-fill (DR-63/DR-64).** That rule is about which QUANTITY is evidence — the delta, never the absolute holding — and it is unchanged; what one un-refreshed read cannot establish is that the quantity has stopped moving. A transport error (the `urllib3.ProtocolError`/`ConnectionError`/`ReadTimeoutError` classes `_http._TRANSIENT_NETWORK_ERRORS` lists) can be raised milliseconds after the exchange FILLED the order, and the positions ledger is read-after-write lagged, so the first post-failure read returns the pre-fill value and `delta == 0`. On the YES leg that sent the reduce-only unwind of a NO leg that was really hedging a live YES fill — selling the hedge, leaving a naked YES position, and reporting `"rolled_back"`, which means nothing of the pair is open; on the NO leg it returned `"failed"` ("nothing to unwind") with a full-size UNHEDGED NO position open and no status in `EXIT_TRADES_NEED_ATTENTION` to escalate it, so the run exited 0. Each ambiguous branch therefore re-reads its own ticker ONCE after `trader._V2_MAPPING_RECHECK_DELAY_SECONDS` and judges the RE-READ — the same reasoning `_confirm_v2_no_mapping` already applies (DR-21) and `v2_probe` applies twice over (DR-60, DR-20). An unreadable re-read is the existing unattributable case, i.e. `manual_review` with no order submitted. The NO leg's re-read is SINGLE-SHOT (`_position_count_once`) because it sits in the unhedged window; the YES leg's is the ordinary retried `_position_count`, matching the first read beside it. Both re-reads, like both first reads, stay OUTSIDE the `except` clause for the `__context__` reason above.

**`TradeResult.status` vocabulary** (the legs below are the SUBMISSION legs from `trader._ordered_legs(spec)` — the NO leg first, then the YES leg; which market each sits on depends on the pair type): `"executed"` (both legs filled, or the YES leg errored but the position delta was exactly `+yes_leg.count`), `"simulated"` (dry run), `"failed"` (the NO leg confirmed unfilled — a clean FoK rejection, which includes the exchange's HTTP 409 kill response, or an error with a delta of 0 **on both the first read and the lag re-read**, DR-64; nothing to unwind — and, with nothing sent at all, a pair stopped because the V2 NO-leg mapping was disproven earlier in the process, whose error reads `NO leg not sent: V2 NO-leg mapping disproven earlier in this run; nothing submitted`, and an add-on whose held pair no longer matched it just before its NO leg, whose error reads `not sent: this does not match the held pair it adds to (<reason>); nothing submitted` — see the paragraph after DR-77's), `"rolled_back"` (the YES leg confirmed unfilled — FoK rejection, or an error with a delta of 0 **on both readings**, DR-63 — and the NO leg's immediate-or-cancel unwind of this pair's NO contracts filled in full; anything the account held on that market before the pair is left as it was), `"rollback_failed"` (the unwind did not fill, or — because the immediate-or-cancel unwind can stop part-way — closed only part of this pair's NO contracts; what is left open is an orphaned position. When the unwind's own 2xx response reports a fill count strictly between zero and the NO leg's count N, the CRITICAL and the error text name exactly how many of this pair's NO contracts are still open, N − fill_count — the error ends `; <N − fill_count> of <N> NO contracts still open`, and the CRITICAL still says to check the account; when that count cannot be known — an error response, a transport error, a body that is not JSON or not a JSON object, or a fill count that is missing, not a finite number, below zero, above N, or too long to subtract exactly at decimal's default 28 digits — the CRITICAL says "up to N". A 2xx fill count of exactly zero is a clean `"canceled"`, whose CRITICAL names all N as open), `"manual_review"` (a leg errored and its outcome could NOT be attributed to this order — either position lookup failed, or the position moved by an amount the order can't explain — or the NO-leg mapping was disproven by `_confirm_v2_no_mapping` after a confirmed NO-leg fill, or a pair's NO leg filled after an earlier disproof). `manual_review` submits no order at all — on the YES leg because unwinding could reverse a real fill, on the NO leg because a reduce-only unwind of a position this order may not own would close an unrelated holding, and on a disproven V2 mapping because the unwind rests on the very hypothesis just disproven (the YES leg is left unsubmitted and the NO leg deliberately left in place). The error strings say "NO leg"/"YES leg" (the ambiguous-but-confirmed YES-leg case keeps the word "ambiguous"), and the prod Excel row's Notes cell is prefixed `[<pair_type>: <SIDE_A> A / <SIDE_B> B[ nB=…] fees=$x.xx[ adds to N held]] ` so a row can be read without knowing which pair type it came from (the last part only on a trade that adds to a held pair). Don't "simplify" `manual_review` into an automatic rollback in either leg. A held position's sale has a vocabulary of its own (`reporter.SaleResult.status`: `sold`, `partly_sold`, `not_sold`, `unbalanced`, `manual_review`, `simulated`; see the live-selling paragraph under Key Patterns), kept apart from these: a sale's status lives on `SaleResult` and is never put on a `TradeResult` (the two vocabularies share only `manual_review` and `simulated`, with the same meaning: a person must check, and nothing was sent), and the trade log words a sale's in its Status column as `sold`, `partly sold`, `not sold`, `unbalanced`, `check` and `simulated`.

**FoK price protection:** Every buy leg must be capped so it fills at or below the scanned price plus a small allowance, or not at all. The V2 endpoint, the only order path, has no "market" order type: a taker order is a marketable fill-or-kill LIMIT, and **the limit price IS the price protection** — `trader._v2_limit_price(leg_kind, scanned_price_dollars, market)` (`leg_kind` is `"buy_yes"`/`"buy_no"`; the scanned price is the leg's `_Leg.price_dollars` — `nA`/`pB` for same-title, `pA`/`nB` for time-series) computes it as the scanned price ceiled onto the market's own tick grid (`scanner.tick_size_for_price()`) plus `BUY_SLIPPAGE_TICKS` ticks, and the buy-NO leg sends the complement `1 − cap` as an ask. The scanned price is **quantized to 6 decimals (`scanner._SCANNED_PRICE_QUANTUM`) before that ceiling**, the same round-before-ceil guard `fee_leg_exact()` applies: every scanned ask level is `1.0 − float(bid)` and 20 of the 99 whole-cent complements land one ULP *above* the exact cent (`1.0 - 0.43 = 0.5700000000000001`), which un-quantized ceils a whole extra tick and hands the FoK limit two ticks of tolerance where one was configured. No Kalshi grid point has a 7th decimal, so the quantize can only remove float noise — it tightens or keeps the cap, never loosens it (TS-03). The final price is then **re-quantized (ceiling) onto the grid of the band that actually contains it** — the cap can step up into a coarser band, or be mirrored across the book by the complement, where the scanned band's grid is no longer valid — and clamped one tick inside the open unit interval on this market's own grid (0.99/0.01 on a cent market, not the finest-grid 0.9999/0.0001). Ceiling is chosen because a floor could round a YES cap BELOW the scanned price and make the order structurally unfillable. It is **not** purely protective, and this file used to claim it was: when the final price lands in a COARSER band than the one it was computed on, ceiling moves it AWAY from the scanned price and LOOSENS the cap by up to one destination-band tick (reachable: scanned 0.10 on `tapered_deci_cent` submits 0.11 where 0.101 was intended). That residual is bounded by one tick, is the accepted cost of keeping the order fillable, and is recorded as its own open finding — distinct from TS-10, which fixed the other half by making `tick_size_for_price` resolve a boundary price to the FINEST containing band instead of the first one. Any new order-building code must carry this cap.


**Order-book LEVELS and MARKET eligibility use different price bounds, and must keep doing so (TS-14).** `scanner._bids_to_ask_levels` drops a level whose complement falls outside `config.MIN_ACTIVE_PRICE_DOLLARS`/`MAX_ACTIVE_PRICE_DOLLARS` (`0.0001`/`0.9999`) — the extreme tradeable levels on Kalshi's FINEST grid. It used to use the 0.01/0.99 MARKET-eligibility bound instead, which silently discarded genuine depth on exactly the regimes whose point is sub-cent ticks: on a `deci_cent` or `center_deci_edge_centi_cent` book, every level under a cent or over 99c vanished before pairing, so a full book reached the sizer as a thin one. The drops are now counted and reported as one summary WARNING per book naming the ticker and the count (silent at zero) — the same idiom as the trading-inactive shard skip count — because invisible drops read downstream as genuinely thin depth. **`scanner._MIN_ACTIVE_PRICE`/`_MAX_ACTIVE_PRICE` (0.01/0.99) did NOT move and must not be unified with the level bound**: they answer a different question — "is this MARKET worth trading at all" — where a sub-cent YES ask means a near-settled market, and widening them would turn a 0.9999 quote into a $0.0001 hedge leg. `backtester.py`'s two mirrors of that market bound stay in step with it for the same reason. Widening the market half is a recorded, deliberately-held operator decision, not an oversight.
**The rollback carries the same kind of protection on the unwind side.** The unwind may never be an unpriced order: V2 has no market type at all, and an unwind with no price bound would let a book that collapsed between the NO leg's fill and the unwind realize an unbounded loss. The bound — `trader._rollback_floor_cents(no_leg)` — is the NO leg's scanned entry (`no_leg.price_dollars`: `nA` for same-title, `nB` for time-series — the depth-weighted fill after enrichment) less `ROLLBACK_MAX_LOSS_CENTS_PER_CONTRACT` (12), clamped into the API's valid `1..99` cent range, with `round()` applied *before* `int()` because that price is a cent-quantized book price carried as a float (`0.57 → 56.99999999999999`, which `int()` would truncate to 56):
- **The unwind** — a reduce-only YES **bid** (a held NO is a short YES, so closing it is a buy-back), so that bound is a bid **CEILING** of `1 − floor/100`. `trader._v2_rollback_price(no_leg)` **ceiling**-quantizes that cap onto the grid of the band containing it and then clamps it by `_v2_top_of_grid_price(no_leg.market)` (`V2_ROLLBACK_BID_PRICE_DOLLARS` floored onto its own band — 0.99 / 0.999 / 0.9999 by regime; `v2_probe`'s unfillable-ask step submits at exactly that level, and so does its no-mapping step's closing bid — the probe overrides the loss-floored price precisely because it is verifying a side, not a price). The ceiling direction here is deliberate and is the OPPOSITE trade-off from the buy legs: it loosens the loss bound by strictly under one destination-band tick, because a floor-quantized off-grid cap would sit below every valid level in its band and structurally kill every off-grid unwind — the orphaned-position failure this path exists to prevent. **The V2 unwind is `immediate_or_cancel`, not fill-or-kill**, because the endpoint rejects a `reduce_only` order with any other time in force (its docs, verbatim: "Orders with reduce_only set to true will be rejected unless time_in_force is immediate_or_cancel."): it buys back what rests at or under the cap and cancels the rest, never resting on the book, so it can close PART of this pair's NO contracts (its count is always `no_leg.count`, never a position read, so on a market the account held before the pair it never reaches the older contracts). A partial close reports a fill count that is neither the full count nor zero, so `_v2_fill_status` raises; `_rollback_no_leg`'s `except` catches it and returns `rollback_failed`, and no second order is sent — a partial close is never reported as closed. Its CRITICAL names the exact count still open, N − fill_count, read from the unwind's own response (see the Fill classification bullet), and says "up to N NO contracts" only when that count cannot be known. Pinned by `tests/test_trader.py::TestV2ExecuteOne::test_v2_partial_unwind_is_rollback_failed` and `::TestPartialUnwindCount`.

A book past the bound kills the unwind, or the part of it past the bound (the IOC bid closes only what rests at or under its cap), leaving the NO-leg position, or what is left of it, open — reported as `status="rollback_failed"` for manual review (`_rollback_no_leg(client, spec, no_leg, reason, *, before=)`'s ORPHANED POSITION alerts name `no_leg.market.ticker` and `no_leg.count`, so a time-series rollback failure names `market_b`, not `market_a`, and, when `before` — the NO market's position read before the pair — is not zero, that earlier holding, which must stay open), exactly the path an unfilled market unwind already took, so the caller's contract is unchanged and only the loss is now bounded. Don't "simplify" the unwind back to an unpriced (or flat top-of-grid) order.

**Backtest cache semantics:** `fetch_all_settled_markets()` and `fetch_candlesticks()` always persist a fresh fetch to disk even when `use_cache=False`, so `--no-cache` refreshes what the next default run loads. `use_cache` gates only the assembled per-start_date `settled_markets_*` cache; the per-day slice stores under `backtest_cache/archive_days/` and `backtest_cache/live_days/` are consulted regardless (see the sharded-fetch gotcha for why they can't go stale). **Since SS-1's Commit C the assembled cache is streamed:** it is named `settled_markets_<start_date>[_<prefilter_tag>][_nomve].jsonl.gz`, written in the day slices' `"jsonl-v1"` framing through `_DayStreamWriter` (atomic tmp+rename, so it is either absent or complete) with a meta block that repeats its identity (`historical._assembled_cache_meta`: kind, start_date, prefilter_tag, include_mve, format — so a file copied onto another request's name is refused), and served as a `SettledCorpus`. On `use_cache=True` the lookup order is: (1) if the `.jsonl.gz` EXISTS, it is validated by ONE full streaming walk — all or nothing: any decode error, truncation, damaged deflate stream or meta mismatch is a WARNING (`Corrupt or mismatched settled-market cache — treating as cache miss`) and a miss that REBUILDS (step 3) — never a fall-through to step 2; a valid one is then served, extended or rebuilt (below); (2) only when NO `.jsonl.gz` exists, a LEGACY `settled_markets_<same stem>.json` — the single JSON document every run before Commit C wrote, GB-scale (measured 2026-09-24: the 2026-05-01 one is 1.30 GB, the largest, 2026-09-14, 3.27 GB) — is NOT served (since 2026-10): it records neither its archive cutoff nor where its live part ends, so it cannot be brought up to date, and the window is re-assembled in full, the file not even read (one INFO line says so); (3) the fetch. New runs never write the legacy format. **A committed rebuild RETIRES the legacy file of the same stem (SS-1 review, fixed 2026-09-24):** once the `.jsonl.gz` is committed, `historical._retire_legacy_cache` deletes `settled_markets_<same stem>.json` (INFO `Removed the superseded legacy settled-market cache …`; a failed delete is a WARNING telling the operator to delete it by hand, and the run goes on). That restored the old invariant — the old code's rebuild OVERWROTE that file, so a superseded assembly could never come back — and destroys exactly what that overwrite destroyed, only after the replacement is on disk (a rebuild that raises retires nothing). Before it the stale legacy corpus — typically the one the BS-02 or subtitle-drift remedy had just rebuilt — was served again whenever the `.jsonl.gz` was damaged (after a WARNING claiming a cache miss) or missing (silently; until 2026-09-24 this repo lived in iCloud-synced `~/Documents`, where iCloud reverted a committed rename — a failed delete or a hand restore can do the same). Since a legacy file is no longer served at all, one that lingers costs only disk; rule (1)'s no-fall-through stays. Don't reintroduce either fall-through. Pinned by `tests/test_historical.py::TestStreamedAssembledCache` (damaged, vanished and rename-reverted streamed caches with a legacy file present; a legacy cache rebuilt and retired; retirement only after commit; the failed-delete WARNING). One `Loaded %d … from cache` line reports a cache served as it is — worded `eligible markets` when the cache was assembled under a prefilter (the backtester's always is) and followed by the assembly's counts when the cache recorded them, or by a statement that it recorded none (M9, `historical._log_cache_load`); without a prefilter it still begins `Loaded %d settled markets from cache`. **Every run is brought up to date: a cache is served as it is only on the UTC day it was assembled or last extended, and an older one is EXTENDED (2026-10).** This replaces §4 of the 2026-09-15 fix plan ("staleness is announced, not enforced"), at the operator's request of 2026-10-02 that every backtest run through the most recent available date. An assembled cache is a snapshot: it holds no market settled after it was assembled (the 2026-09-17 cache's meta says `assembled_at` 2026-09-24T12:37:49Z, and its first record — the live frontier is served newest-settled first — settled at 12:18:38Z that same day, a partial frontier day), while every Period line says the window runs to today. Its meta block carries informational keys the identity check never compares (`_assembled_cache_meta` does not name them, so a cache written before any of them existed is still read): `assembled_at`, `archive_cutoff_ts` (P2 — the cutoff the assembly read), `assembly_counts` (M9 — see the prefilter gotcha), `live_frontier_ts` (the first second of the partial UTC day the corpus was assembled, or last extended, on — the day a later run extends from) and, on an extended cache, `full_assembly_at` (when it was last assembled in full). `SettledCorpus.open_validated` captures the whole block from its one validating walk (`_day_store_iter(..., meta_out=)`), never from a second read, and `historical._up_to_date_corpus` is the ONE place the rule is decided for a valid cache: its frontier day is today (UTC) — served as it is, with ZERO network calls, announced by one INFO line (`_announce_cache_hit`: its assembly time and age, the cutoff at assembly, that the first run on a later UTC day extends it, and that `--no-cache` re-assembles it in full now); an earlier day — one `/historical/cutoff` read, and when the cutoff is the one the cache was assembled under, or has moved forward but not past its frontier day, the cache is EXTENDED (below); the cutoff moved past the frontier day (markets the extension would need have migrated into the archive, which only a full assembly reads), moved backward, a frontier day or assembly time after today by the host's clock, or a cache recording no assembly time or no cutoff — re-assembled in full, each with an INFO line naming why (that re-assembly is an ordinary miss: if it fails, the error propagates as a miss's does, and the old cache is not served); the cutoff read or the extension itself failing — a WARNING naming the failure, and the cache is served as it was, with `CorpusProvenance.stale` set (`_served_stale`) so the closing log line and the page call it an earlier day's cache that could not be brought up to date, never today's. A cache written before `live_frontier_ts` existed counts as today's when it was assembled today, and is otherwise extended from the day BEFORE its assembly day, or from the day its newest live record settled on when that is earlier (`_first_live_settle`, one walk of its archive part): an assembly that ran across one or more UTC midnights between its live fetch and its stamp captured its frontier day only partly, extending from too late a day would drop the rest of that day for good, and a day slice already on disk costs a read, not a fetch (pinned by `::test_a_stamp_less_cache_whose_assembly_took_two_days_loses_nothing`). Every epoch value of the meta block is range-checked (`_meta_epoch`; `_parse_assembled_at` likewise), so a value no datetime can hold reads as unrecorded and costs a re-assembly, never an exception (`::test_a_meta_value_no_datetime_holds_never_raises`). **The extension** (`historical._extend_assembled_cache`) prunes the live day slices wholly before the current cutoff (`_prune_stale_live_days`), fetches the live endpoint's settled days from the frontier day (or `start_date`, if later) through now (`_fetch_live_phase` — the persisted `live_days/` slices reused, only the missing days and today's frontier fetched) and re-assembles from three sources in a fresh assembly's order, walked twice like a fresh assembly (count and collect titles, then patch and write, held to the same identity check): the old cache's archive part (its records settled before the cutoff it was assembled under), the new live records, and the old cache's later records settled before the frontier day, or exactly at its midnight — its other frontier-day records are dropped, since the complete day replaces them (`_CorpusSplit`, one read of the old file per walk). The midnight second is kept because the new fetch asks from it and an endpoint that reads `min_settled_ts` as exclusive would leave a record settled exactly then out; a copy the new fetch does hold comes first and wins the dedup (`::test_a_record_settled_at_the_frontier_midnight_survives_an_exclusive_min`). With the cutoff unchanged the result is exactly a fresh assembly's records in a fresh assembly's order (pinned by `tests/test_historical.py::TestCacheExtension::test_a_next_day_hit_extends_the_cache_to_equal_a_fresh_assembly` and `::test_with_a_prefilter_it_still_equals_a_fresh_assembly`); with a cutoff that moved forward but not past the frontier day, the same markets, the ones that migrated into the archive taken from the old live part. Titles are resolved for the new records and for old non-combo records whose title is still blank (`_needs_title`); every other old record keeps the title it was assembled with. The new file is written beside the old one through `_DayStreamWriter` and renamed over it only after the second walk reproduced the first; its meta records the new `assembled_at`, cutoff and frontier day, and `full_assembly_at`, and NO `assembly_counts` — the dropped frontier day's counts cannot be separated from the rest — so the extension logs its own counts instead. **Cost, an estimate (not measured live — this container cannot reach Kalshi):** each newly completed settled day is fetched once — at today's combo-heavy volumes an hour or two of paging per full day, days fetched in parallel (the 7-day window's seven full-volume days took 1 h 47 min) — plus today's partial day; a completed day is then stored as a live day slice and shared by every window. DR-13's EMPTY-cache age rule (`EMPTY_ASSEMBLED_CACHE_MAX_AGE_SECONDS`) went with the old serving path: an empty streamed cache is served on its own day and extended like any other later, and an empty legacy one is re-assembled like any legacy file. `historical._corpus_provenance` — one pure function of the block, applied to the block just written on a fresh assembly or an extension and to the block read back on a hit — builds a frozen `CorpusProvenance(from_cache, assembled_at, archive_cutoff, assembly_counts, full_assembly_at, stale)` carried on the returned `SettledCorpus` — the only kind of corpus the fetch returns now (`LegacySettledCorpus` went with the legacy serving path). It rides `backtester._prepare_candidates` (read BY TYPE off a `SettledCorpus`, never by attribute probing) → `_Candidates.corpus_provenance` → `BacktestSweep.corpus_provenance`, defaulted like `label_coverage`, and is rendered by `dashboard._corpus_provenance_html` directly under the Period line on EVERY run (DR-66: a healthy run shows its assembly time too) and by `backtest._log_corpus_provenance(sweep)` as the run's closing line: assembled by this run, extended through today by this run (naming the full assembly it extends), served from an earlier run's cache assembled earlier today, or served as an earlier day's cache that could not be brought up to date; the archive cutoff at assembly is information only. `None` — the Monday-feasibility short-circuit, a test that stubs the fetch with a plain list, a hand-built sweep, no sweep at all — renders "not recorded". Pinned by `tests/test_historical.py::TestCorpusProvenance` (whose hits run with clients and a signed GET that FAIL the test on any access) and `::TestCacheExtension`, `tests/test_backtester.py::TestCorpusProvenanceIsCarried`, `tests/test_dashboard.py::TestCorpusProvenanceHeader` and `tests/test_backtest.py::TestCorpusProvenanceLine`. The page header is outside `TestGoldenSections`' digests, so no golden moved. The outcome-label census WARNING (`backtester._report_outcome_label_coverage`) and the dashboard's below-floor banner (`dashboard._label_coverage_html`) name BOTH spellings in their "equivalently, also delete the assembled cache" clause — the streamed `settled_markets_*.jsonl.gz` and any legacy `settled_markets_*.json` — because naming the `.json` alone, as they did until the SS-1 follow-up, left the streamed cache to be served again. That banner's `tests/fixtures/dashboard_golden_main.json` digest was deliberately re-captured after `main` @ `fe0a758` (the fixture's `amended` entry names it, beside the other deliberate re-captures listed there). Every component of the name is part of the RESULT's identity, and the DR-57/prefilter-tag rules below apply unchanged to both formats (they share the name stem). When a `prefilter` is passed, the tag is appended — a prefiltered result is a strict subset, so it must never be served to an unfiltered caller or to one using different filter semantics. **`INCLUDE_MVE_MARKETS=False` appends `_nomve` (DR-57).** The flag changes WHAT IS FETCHED, not just how markets are grouped: it adds `mve_filter="exclude"` to the archive query and to every live page (three sites in `historical.py`), so an unkeyed cache served an MVE-INCLUSIVE assembly to a run configured to exclude MVE, and vice versa — severe in practice because ~99.7% of the assembled cache is MVE combo markets, so an `INCLUDE_MVE_MARKETS=False` run analysed an almost entirely MVE corpus while believing it had excluded them, with nothing abnormal in the output. **Only the False case is marked, deliberately:** every assembled cache currently on disk was built with MVE included (there is a 3.27 GB one), so the default `True` filename stays byte-identical to the pre-DR-57 name and no existing cache is orphaned into a multi-hour refetch — don't "tidy" this into a symmetric `_mve`/`_nomve` pair. The per-day slice stores need no such marker: their meta already carries `include_mve`, so a flag flip invalidates them on the existing meta check — at the cost of refetching and OVERWRITING every slice on each flip, since the slice PATHS themselves are not flag-keyed (before DR-57 the assembled-cache hit masked this, so a flip never reached the slices at all; now a flipped run re-pays the day fetch, and so does the next run that flips back and misses its assembled cache). `_prepare_candidates()` (the band- and k-independent prologue shared by `run_backtest()`, through `_prepare_entries()`, and `run_backtest_sweep()`) passes `backtester._prefilter_cache_tag()`: `config.SETTLED_PREFILTER_CACHE_TAG`, which names the version of `backtester._can_ever_enter`'s logic, joined to `SCHEDULED_RUN.cache_slug()` — `checkpoint-v3-mon0900-America-Los_Angeles` on the shipped schedule. **The tag is built when it is used, from the same `backtester.SCHEDULED_RUN` binding the predicate reads**, so the predicate and the tag always name one schedule: a changed or patched schedule re-keys the cache by itself, and a harness that patches the schedule around a fetch can never write a corpus filtered for one schedule under another's name (a tag fixed at import could). **Bump the literal whenever that predicate's logic changes, or `CANDLESTICK_PERIOD_INTERVAL_MINUTES` does** (the predicate floors each checkpoint onto the candle period and the tag does not name it), or a stale prefiltered cache is silently reused. **P5 bumped it (`monday-eligibility-v1` → `monday-checkpoint-v2`, the checkpoint-instant predicate — see the prefilter gotcha), which ORPHANS every assembled cache written under v1:** all eight on disk on 2026-09-24 — `settled_markets_<date>_monday-eligibility-v1.*` for 2026-05-01, 05-28, 07-13 (the three pre-cutoff ones), 08-29, 09-05, 09-07, 09-14 and 09-17, 8,692,223,608 bytes in all. They are never read again and, because the NAME STEM changed, never retired either (`_retire_legacy_cache` deletes only a legacy file of the rebuild's own stem), so they sit in `backtest_cache/` until deleted by hand. The day slices are not keyed by the tag and are untouched.
