None of the eight held a usable RESULT: the three pre-cutoff ones were assembled on 2026-08-03, before the subtitle fix, and group strike-blind (the 2026-05-01 one at 1.86% subtitle coverage — see `BACKTEST_OUTCOME_LABEL_WARN_FRACTION`'s comment — and head samples of the other two are almost entirely `null`), which this file already says to rebuild; the 2-byte 2026-08-29 one is empty and more than a day old, which DR-13's empty-cache rule (since retired) treated as a miss; and the other four start after the 2026-07-25 cutoff, so they were 0-trade by construction under that day's code (since 2026-10 such a window's markets are priced from the live candlestick endpoint). What the orphaning costs is the next run of each start date. A post-cutoff window re-assembles from the live day slices on disk plus the frontier day (the 7-day window's fresh run took 2,382 s against 1,040 s from its cache — about 22 minutes more), and fetches any live day not on disk. A PRE-cutoff window's next run is close to a full fetch rather than a cache hit, and its duration is UNMEASURED: on 2026-09-24 it needed 85 / 58 / 12 archive created-days (start 05-01 / 05-28 / 07-13), none of them valid on disk — the 221 slices for 2025-10-15..2026-05-27 were stamped with the 2026-06-04 cutoff, the 36 for 2026-05-28..07-02 with 2026-07-03, against the 2026-07-25 cutoff read that day, and 2026-07-03..07-24 had no slice — plus 35 of its 61 past live days (07-25..09-23; 08-03..08-28, 08-31..09-04, 09-12, 09-13, 09-15 and 09-16 are not on disk), the frontier and the tail walk (since 2026-10-01 the archive phase reads every created-day from 2021-06-01 instead of walking a tail). The one timing anchor is run 1 of the 2026-09-17 window, whose 7 full-volume live days (22,175,942 records) took 1 h 47 min fetched in parallel (00:30:46 → 02:17:45 in that run's log). That is the rebuild the strike-blind caches needed anyway, and it is not by itself a complete fix: the live slices for 07-25..08-02, 08-29 and 08-30 (written 2026-08-03 and 2026-09-01 UTC) carry `subtitle: null` too and are reused unconditionally until deleted (the subtitle-drift remedy). The chain's own provenance also needs a rebuild to show: a hit on any v1 cache records neither `archive_cutoff_ts` (P2) nor `assembly_counts` (M9, P3), so both read "not recorded" until the start date is re-assembled — which the bump now forces. **The entry-checkpoint change bumped it again (`monday-checkpoint-v2` → `checkpoint-v3`, now with the schedule's slug appended — see the entry-checkpoint gotcha), which ORPHANS every assembled cache written under v2 the same way:** `settled_markets_2025-09-24_monday-checkpoint-v2.jsonl.gz` (841,311,284 bytes) and `settled_markets_2026-09-17_monday-checkpoint-v2.jsonl.gz` (511,174,809 bytes) are never read again by this code and never retired by it (different stem). On the shipped schedule v3 admits a SUPERSET of v2 (the checkpoint is later on the same UTC date), so a v2 corpus could not have been reused anyway: the next run of each start date re-assembles from the day slices, and fetches candles for the newly eligible tickers. Other branches still read the v2 files, so delete them by hand only once no checkout still carries the v2 tag. `prefilter` and `prefilter_tag` must be passed together (`ValueError` otherwise) precisely so an untagged filtered cache can't exist. Candlestick cache files are tagged with the `[open_ts, close_ts]` window, the `period_interval` and the fields version (`CANDLESTICK_CACHE_FIELDS_VERSION`) they cover — a cache whose window starts later than the request (one built for a later `--start-date`) is not reused for an earlier one, while a market that opened after both start dates is requested from its own open (`backtester._candle_window_open`) under either and reuses its cache, and a cache built under a different granularity (e.g. an old pre-hourly daily cache) is not silently reused as if it matched the current `CANDLESTICK_PERIOD_INTERVAL_MINUTES`, and a file written under another fields version is fetched again. Old caches from before this tag existed migrate automatically on the next fetch, same as the legacy bare-list format. **`event_titles_v2.json` is a cross-run accumulator of GENUINE answers, is written as a MERGE, and is RETURNED as one, so `--no-cache` no longer wipes it OR discards it:** `_load_or_build_event_titles` always reads the disk map (even when `use_cache=False`, which still re-resolves every requested ticker), keeps this call's answers in a map of their own, and merges them into the loaded accumulator IN PLACE: disk entries are preserved, a fresh non-empty title wins, a fresh `""` (the poison pill from a failed lookup, or an event a listing returned without a title) never clobbers a non-empty disk title, and a fresh `""` for a ticker disk has never seen is still stored, keeping poison-pill semantics — but a ticker the lookup cap DEFERRED is never stored at all (DR-51; see the event-title gotcha). The file is rewritten only when the call changed it, and never when an accumulator file could not be READ that call: `historical._read_title_file` tells an `OSError` (a permission error, an iCloud file that cannot be downloaded offline — it may read next run) from content that is not a JSON object, and a read failure writes nothing, so it can neither overwrite the v2 file nor commit the migration marker over legacy titles nobody saw (the next run reads it again, or retries the migration). A v2 file whose CONTENT is bad is replaced, as the pre-DR-51 accumulator was. **The legacy `event_titles.json` is migrated once.** It stored `""` for every deferred ticker too — 3,996,906 → 7,986,570 keys (202 → 374 MB) in the one fresh 2026-09-24 7-day run, 7,918,449 of them KXMVE tickers mapped to `""` — and the old code parsed it whole and held two more full copies of it (a seed and a merge) on every fresh fetch, which put the title phase at the top of that run's peak RSS. `_load_event_title_accumulator` keeps exactly the legacy file's non-empty entries (a legacy `""` cannot be told apart from a never-looked-up ticker, so every one is dropped), `_retire_legacy_event_titles` deletes the legacy file once the v2 file is committed, and the v2 file's EXISTENCE is the marker, so a legacy file an older build writes back later is ignored. A legacy file whose content cannot be parsed is WARNed about and left in place, none of its titles are imported, and the v2 file is written as the marker; the WARNING names the recovery (repair it, delete `event_titles_v2.json`, re-run). A legacy file still present beside the v2 file — that one, a delete that failed or was interrupted after the migration, or an older build's (a checkout from before DR-51, sharing this `backtest_cache/`) re-grown accumulator — is never read and never deleted, since in the last case it is that build's live data, but `_warn_if_legacy_lingers` names it and its size in a WARNING on every call until it is gone. Measured with a synthetic-accumulator harness (a fresh process per call, network mocked, peak RSS over the caller's request set): with 2,000,000 accumulated keys and a 2,000,000-ticker request the pre-DR-51 call added 1,075 MiB, and 1,657 MiB on the next 2,000,000-ticker window (its accumulator by then 286.7 MB); the post-DR-51 call added 497 MiB on its one-time migration and 85 MiB on the next window (accumulator 1.38 MB). The one-time migration of a 7,986,570-key legacy file added 1,412 and 1,424 MiB (two runs) over a 1,000,000-ticker request. Read these as magnitudes: identical fresh-process runs vary — three runs of a 4,000,000-key migration over a 4,000,000-ticker request added 561, 855 and 916 MiB. What a dropped legacy `""` re-opens: nothing unless a later window asks for that ticker. Only 16 of them were non-combo; a re-asked combo is deferred again, never looked up, unless that window's whole miss set fits under the cap. The one standing cost is that a cache-miss re-fetch of an already-seen window no longer returns early — its combo tickers are unknown again, so the bulk listings page to their barren bail-out (the bulk listings took 36 s on the 2026-09-24 fresh fetch, a 2,382 s run). **The RETURN is that merged view restricted to the caller's tickers** (the in-place-merged accumulator read once per requested ticker), not this run's resolution alone. Returning `cached` discarded the accumulator at the last step: under `--no-cache` every ticker the listings missed and the `EVENT_TITLE_FALLBACK_MAX_LOOKUPS` cap skipped came back as the `""` pill even though disk held a real title — 771,601 unresolved against a 5,000 cap, so ~99% of stragglers. Those markets then group by market title alone, collapsing `(event_title, title, subtitle)` toward the bare title (measured: 52 groups with titles vs 133, largest 112, without), which is the direction that manufactures cross-event false positives under the 95% co-resolution prior (TS-11). Restricted to the requested tickers because the accumulator holds every ticker every past run ever resolved; a caller asking about forty must not receive eight hundred thousand.

**The Monday-feasibility pre-check measures its window in UTC.** `_prepare_candidates` decides whether any entry checkpoint (a `SCHEDULED_RUN` weekday — see the entry-checkpoint gotcha; the checkpoint's time of day is the live run's instant, 16:00 or 17:00 UTC, but the window's dates stay UTC dates) exists in `[start_date, today]`, and `today` is `datetime.now(UTC).date()` — never `date.today()`, which is LOCAL. West of UTC the local date lags for the first hours of each UTC day (7 of every 24 on a PDT host), so a window whose only Monday IS the current UTC day short-circuited to zero trades and reported the run as structurally impossible when it was not (TS-13). `_build_equity_curve` already used UTC for the same reason; `backtest.py`'s "Period:" line and `dashboard.py`'s rendered period follow, or they print a span the run did not simulate. Tests freeze `backtester.datetime.now(tz)`, not `backtester.date.today()` — the seam moved with the fix.

**Every backtest window runs through today: a market settled after the archive cutoff is priced from Kalshi's live candlestick endpoint (2026-10).** Markets that settled after Kalshi's archive cutoff 404 on `/historical/markets/{ticker}/candlesticks` (verified live 2026-08-03: a `--start-date` after the cutoff, then 2026-06-04, produced 404s for every one of its pair tickers), and the cutoff trails today by about two months (2026-07-29 when read on 2026-09-28), so until this change no trade could enter on a market of the last two months of any window, and a window starting after the cutoff was structurally 0-trade. Kalshi's docs send such markets to `GET /series/{series_ticker}/markets/{ticker}/candlesticks`, which nothing called. `historical.fetch_candlesticks(..., *, series=None, live_first=False)` now asks both (`_candle_endpoints`): given the market's LITERAL series (`historical.series_ticker` of its event ticker — never `scanner.event_series`, which collapses the `KXMVE*` family), the endpoint `live_first` names first and, on a 404 from it, the other for the whole window; any other failure, or a 404 from both, is final (one line, `[]`, nothing cached), and without a series only the archive is asked, as before. Paging, parsing and caching are unchanged; the live endpoint's per-request cap is ASSUMED to be the archive's `CANDLESTICK_MAX_CANDLES_PER_REQUEST` (not measured). `backtester._fetch_candles_parallel(..., *, cutoff_ts=None)` routes each market by its settlement against the corpus's archive cutoff (`_settled_after`: live first for a market settled at or after it; `_prepare_candidates` passes `_Candidates.corpus_provenance.archive_cutoff`), which only saves requests — the 404 fallback makes the routing result-neutral, so a cutoff that moved since the corpus was assembled costs a request, never a series. **The post-cutoff "structurally 0-trade" verdict went with the defect it described:** `historical._starts_at_or_after_cutoff`, `_warn_post_cutoff`, `CorpusProvenance.post_cutoff` and its repeat on a cache hit, `backtest.py`'s closing verdict WARNING, the dashboard's red banner and amber stale-verdict line, and `backtester.max_trades_simulated` and `dashboard._MaxTrades` (whose only use was that verdict) are all gone; the archive cutoff is still reported, as information, on the corpus line of the log and the page, and DR-50's planned short-circuit of a post-cutoff window is moot. **Not verified live** (this container cannot reach Kalshi): that the live endpoint serves a settled market's whole history, and its request cap — the first backtest after this change should show the `returned no candles` count falling and trades appearing in the window's last two months. A ticker NEITHER endpoint serves is still not cached (a poisoned cache would silence it forever, including after its data migrates into the archive), so it is re-paid on every run, in parallel. **Because they are re-paid every run, each one must be cheap to log (TS-02).** `historical.fetch_candlesticks` logs a failure as ONE line — `HTTP <status> <reason>` via `historical._exception_summary()`, never the SDK's `ApiException.__str__`, which emits the status, the reason, the ENTIRE HTTP header dict and the body across five lines (~900 bytes) and whose own first line is only `(404)`. `backtester._fetch_candles_parallel` then reports the per-run count once, just before it returns: `N of M tickers returned no candles`, counted off the RESULT dict (there is no exception to count — `fetch_candlesticks` already fail-softs to `[]`) and deliberately outside its `if work:` block so tickers resolved to `[]` for a missing/unparseable `close_time` are included, silent at zero. `historical._lookup_one`'s event-title failure takes the identical one-line treatment, for the same reason: it fires once per unresolved ticker, up to `EVENT_TITLE_FALLBACK_MAX_LOOKUPS` of them. Don't log an SDK exception whole on any per-ticker path — nor on the order path: `trader.py` logs and records (`TradeResult.error`, hence the trade log's Notes cell) every failed order submission, unwind, position read, balance re-read and transfer through `_http.api_error_summary`, one line with the HTTP status, reason and the exchange's error `code`, `message` and `details` (a live run on 2026-09-28 wrote seven ~900-byte multi-line errors before it), `scanner._fetch_orderbook` logs a failed order-book read — per ticker, and inside `pre_execution_check` — the same way, and `tests/test_trader.py::TestFailedRequestsAreLoggedOnOneLine` pins by AST that every exception `trader.py` catches by name is either re-raised or handed only to `api_error_summary`, `_is_fok_kill` or `_partial_unwind_counts` (the two multi-line exceptions: the logs of an unexpected crash in `execute_trades` and in `sell_positions` keep their traceback, `exc_info=True`). Other modules still log an exception whole at a few once-per-run sites (`main._run_prod`'s post-trade balance read, `scanner.fetch_shard_statuses`) and `v2_probe` prints its to the terminal; `historical._exception_summary` and `scanner._error_text` are older one-line describers with their own pinned formats. One ordinary backtest emitted hundreds of MB into `kalshi_backtest.log`'s 20 MB × 3 rotation, 99.5% of the file was that single warning, and the rotation evicted ~419 MB of history including the shard census, prefilter counts and calibration table the same runs had written.

**Daily candlesticks silently produced zero data for most markets — use hourly.** `historical.fetch_candlesticks()` requests `period_interval=CANDLESTICK_PERIOD_INTERVAL_MINUTES` (60, hourly), not daily (1440). Confirmed 2026-07 by direct API testing: `/historical/markets/{ticker}/candlesticks` with `period_interval=1440` only emits a bar for a market whose lifespan crosses a UTC midnight boundary — a market open for 2 hours entirely within one day returned 0 daily candles but 2 hourly candles for the same window. Most Kalshi markets are single-game/few-hour windows within one calendar day, so daily granularity structurally produced zero price data for most markets regardless of liquidity or age, which is why backtest runs kept finding 0 trades even after the pair-extraction logic itself was verified correct. `period_interval=1` (minute) returns HTTP 400 — hourly is the finest granularity actually available. `_candle_at_or_before()` and `_monday_timestamps()` in `backtester.py` are granularity-agnostic (they just scan sorted candles for the latest one at-or-before a timestamp), so switching interval required no change to the entry-detection logic — only the fetch call and the cache tag. Candles are fetched in parallel across tickers (`backtester._fetch_candles_parallel`, `CANDLESTICK_FETCH_MAX_WORKERS`); each worker still paces its own pages with `fetch_candlesticks`'s unchanged `rate_limit_sleep` default (0.15s), so the per-connection rate is the same as before — only the number of connections changed.

**Backtester pair-extraction eligibility prefilter — don't remove it.** At current Kalshi volumes, normalized-title groups can collapse tens of thousands of hourly/intraday markets into one group (a live measurement found a 53k-member hourly-crypto group in a 3-month window), making the naive O(n²) pairing in `_extract_pairs()` infeasible (500B+ iterations observed, hung for over an hour). `_prepare_candidates()` (shared by `run_backtest()`, through `_prepare_entries()`, and `run_backtest_sweep()`) filters `markets` through `backtester._can_ever_enter(m, start_date)` before either grouping call: `_find_entry()` can only open a trade at an entry checkpoint — the weekly live run's instant, `config.SCHEDULED_RUN` (Monday 09:00 America/Los_Angeles: 16:00 UTC under daylight time, 17:00 UTC under standard time; see the entry-checkpoint gotcha) — whose DATE lies in `[start_date, min(close_a, close_b) − 1 day]` and needs an hourly candle at-or-before that checkpoint for both legs; a candle's `ts` is its period END and a market has no candle ending at or before the start of the hour it opened in, so each leg must have OPENED BEFORE THE START OF THE CHECKPOINT'S CANDLE PERIOD (`backtester._checkpoint_floor`; on the shipped schedule, whose checkpoints fall on the hour, that is the checkpoint instant itself) — a market with no such checkpoint on/after `start_date` and dated on or before its own close date minus one day can never appear in any entered pair, as either leg, in either pair type, and can be safely dropped before grouping. **The lower bound is the checkpoint INSTANT, not the opening DATE (M8 of the 2026-09-24 review, fixed in P5).** The predicate used to compare `open_time` by date, so it kept every market that opened later on the checkpoint Monday itself — none of which `_find_entry` can enter at that checkpoint: 2,192,241 of the 7,274,215 records of the 2026-09-17 7-day window's assembled cache (30.1%, the review's streamed `open_time` histogram), and — contrary to the review's expectation that a multi-week window would dilute it — 336,750 of the 570,506 records of the 4-Monday 2026-07-13 legacy cache (59.0%, measured for P5 by streaming that file), every one opened on a Monday at or after 09:00 UTC. `ScheduledRun.instant` on `backtester.SCHEDULED_RUN` is the ONE definition of the checkpoint instant since the entry-checkpoint change (under P5 it was `backtester._checkpoint_datetime`'s 09:00 UTC), read by `_monday_timestamps` (the scan) through `_checkpoint_datetime` and by `_can_ever_enter` (the prefilter) through `_checkpoint_floor(SCHEDULED_RUN, d)`, which floors that same instant, both on `SCHEDULED_RUN`'s weekday (pinned by `tests/test_backtester.py::TestOneCheckpointDefinition`); the upper bound is still `close_time`'s DATE minus a day, exactly as `_find_entry` builds `scan_end`, and a naive `open_time` (no offset — only a hand-edited cache) keeps the date test, as does an aware one whose UTC instant falls outside `datetime`'s range (a year-1 time east of UTC, a year-9999 one west of it, where `astimezone(UTC)` raises `OverflowError`), and a checkpoint that cannot be placed in that range (`_checkpoint_floor` returns `None`) counts as reachable on the opening date, so the close-date bound alone decides. The rest is done on date DIFFERENCES, never a date plus a `timedelta`, so the predicate never raises on any parseable timestamp — it runs as the fetch's assembly prefilter (on the frontier's worker thread too) and in both of `_prepare_candidates`' passes, where an exception would end the run rather than keep one absurd record; the date test it replaced could raise near either end of the range (`close − 1 day` on a year-1 close, its advance-to-Monday loop past 9999-12-31). **The boundary is measured, and it is tight.** Re-measured for P5 on the DR-73 calibration corpus's cached candle series (3,542 series, 952,519 hourly candles): 0 candles end at or before the start of their market's opening hour — including the 573 series whose request opened before the market did, so the endpoint had the chance to serve one — and at 13,239 Monday checkpoints a candle at or before the checkpoint always meant an opening strictly before it; 1,233 of the 1,310 series whose market opened off the hour have a first candle ending at the next top of the hour, so a market opened at 08:30 CAN enter at 09:00 and the rule cannot be tightened past `open_time < checkpoint` — past `open_time <` the start of the checkpoint's candle period, for a schedule whose instant is off the hour (`_checkpoint_floor`; the scan's own test of what it can reach is parametrized over such schedules). On the 2026-07-13 cache, 11,098 of the markets P5 drops have a cached candle series (138,240 candles), and none has a candle at or before any checkpoint it could have been scanned at. That rule is an API behaviour, not a documented contract — the date test relied on a weaker form of it — so if a series ever shows a candle ending before its opening hour, revisit this predicate. **Result-neutral for a given corpus, and pinned three ways.** Under P5's 09:00 UTC checkpoint the checkpoint-instant set was a SUBSET of the date-granular one for every real UTC offset (the date test read the date in the string's own offset; a UTC date trailing the local one means a UTC time of 10:00 or later, past that Monday's checkpoint). Under v3's 16:00/17:00 UTC checkpoint it is a subset only for openings stamped at or west of +07:00 (+08:00 while Los Angeles is on daylight time), which covers every Kalshi timestamp (all `Z`) — at an offset X a Tuesday local date is the Monday in UTC from 24:00 − X, which falls before the 17:00 UTC checkpoint once X passes 7 hours (before 16:00 once it passes 8), so further east v3 admits what the date test dropped; and on a fixed corpus the candidate list holds exactly the old pairs minus every pair with a dropped leg — nothing is added, and each group's own pairs keep their order — but a group whose FIRST eligible member was dropped now first appears later in the eligible stream, so its pairs can move later in the list (measured on the real 2026-05-01 corpus: 826 of 2,054 pairs kept, 9 order inversions, the same 3 trades); every removed pair's `_find_entry` is `None`, every kept pair's is identical, and `run_backtest`'s trades and equity curve are identical — list order reaches a result only through `_simulate_at_discount`'s tie-breaks, i.e. two candidates from different groups tied EXACTLY on entry date, `entry_monthly_ratio` and pair type, whose listing order can swap and, only if cash binds between them, which one is funded — `tests/test_backtester.py::TestCanEverEnterAtTheCheckpointInstant` (the boundary to the second, UTC offsets, both clock changes, the naive fallback, and both ends of `datetime`'s range — never an exception in any offset, and, for openings stamped at or west of +07:00, still a subset of the date test there), `::TestCanEverEnterMatchesTheScan` (exact equality, over a grid of about 152,000 points — 151,928 on the shipped schedule — spanning both clock changes, under five schedules — the shipped one, Monday 09:00 UTC, Kolkata 09:00 (03:30 UTC, off the hour), a Los Angeles Thursday 09:30 and Tokyo 09:00 (00:00 UTC) — with an oracle built from `_monday_timestamps` and the measured candle rule, plus the subset property on openings at or west of +07:00 and `test_v3_admits_everything_v2_did`, the Monday 09:00 UTC predicate verbatim as a reference) and `::TestCheckpointPrefilterIsResultNeutral` (the real `_prepare_candidates` / `_entries_for_band` / `run_backtest`, ladders on and off, the old predicate patched in as the baseline). At scale (P5's scratch harness, two 30,000-market synthetic corpora, ladders on and off): 1,663–1,819 candidate pairs removed per run, all `None`; 2,508–3,151 entries and 20 trades identical; candle-fetch tickers down by about 30% (10,758 → 7,358 to 12,286 → 8,716). On the real 2026-07-13 corpus, streamed through the real `_prepare_candidates` with only locally cached candles (no network): 273 → 170 candidate pairs, the 103 removed all `None` (22 of them with candle series on both legs), 0 entries either way, and `_prepare_candidates` plus every pair's `_find_entry` took 43.4–43.9 s → 20.0–20.3 s at a 465–493 MiB peak. That corpus was assembled on 2026-08-03, before the subtitle fix (5,983 of the 6,051 `subtitle` fields in its first 3,000,000 bytes are `null`), so it groups STRIKE-BLIND: its pair counts are not what a healthy corpus forms, and with 0 entries it never exercised a kept pair's entry — that equality rests on the synthetic corpora and the unit tests above, and the real corpus shows only that nothing dropped could have entered. What DOES move is intended: every eligible-market figure (`Markets to analyze`, `Groupable subset`, the outcome-label and deadline-phrasing census denominators, so a `below_floor` verdict can move in an edge case) now counts only markets that could enter — the eligible counts this file quotes from before P5 (7,274,215; 7,260,952; the frontier's 7,190,452) were measured under the date test. One assembly-time effect is not a predicate effect and is recorded rather than excluded: a fresh assembly resolves event titles for fewer tickers, and when its unresolved non-combo set exceeds `EVENT_TITLE_FALLBACK_MAX_LOOKUPS` the cap can reach tickers a v1 assembly never looked up — the same run-to-run variation any two assemblies at different times already have. The predicate costs 0.78 µs a call (0.776–0.784 µs) against the date test's 1.46–1.54 µs, over 300,000 synthetic cache-shaped records (two runs, each timing both predicates twice) — cheaper, because it finds the first Monday by date arithmetic instead of the date test's day-by-day loop. **`config.SETTLED_PREFILTER_CACHE_TAG` went from `monday-eligibility-v1` to `monday-checkpoint-v2` with it, and to `checkpoint-v3` (joined to `SCHEDULED_RUN`'s slug by `backtester._prefilter_cache_tag()`) with the entry-checkpoint change** — see the cache-semantics gotcha for what each bump orphans. `open_time` is carried through `historical._market_to_dict()`; **cache files written before this field existed have no `open_time` and read back as `None`**, and `_can_ever_enter()` treats a missing/unparseable `open_time` or `close_time` as "can't prove ineligibility" (keeps the market, no speedup) rather than risking an incorrect drop — re-run with `--no-cache` to unlock the speedup on old caches. The same predicate is ALSO handed to `fetch_all_settled_markets(prefilter=...)` so ineligible records are dropped during assembly instead of being materialized and cached first — at full-MVE volumes the unfiltered list is the difference between a working run and an OOM, and it also shrinks the `_load_or_build_event_titles` ticker set from runaway to bounded. This is result-neutral by construction (filtering during an order-preserving merge yields the same subsequence as filtering afterwards; `test_prefilter_assembly_equals_postfilter` asserts exact list equality), and the day-slice FILES stay complete and unfiltered because they're shared across start dates. **Keep `_prepare_candidates()`'s own post-fetch filter anyway** — it's idempotent, is applied inside both of its walks over the corpus at no extra pass (SS-1), and keeps the guarantee local to the O(n²) pairing that depends on it. **What the prefilter drops is COUNTED and REPORTED, not only dropped (M9 of the 2026-09-24 review, fixed in P3).** The fetch's lines used to count only its survivors while calling them `settled markets` (`Historical endpoint: %d markets`, `Live endpoint: %d recently settled markets`, `Total settled markets from %s: %d`), and the backtester's re-check, run on a corpus the fetch had already prefiltered, logged `Eligibility prefilter: skipping 0/N` on every production path — the 7-day window printed `skipping 0/7274215` beside `Live endpoint: 7274215 recently settled markets` while about 17.3M of about 24.6M settled records had been dropped (the review's estimate, an upper bound on the prefilter's share since it also covers boundary duplicates and out-of-window records), so a prefilter that worked and one that rejected nothing logged the same lines. The assembly's FIRST walk now counts, per endpoint, every record settled in the window and splits the ones not kept into `rejected` (the prefilter) and `duplicates` (a repeated or blank ticker) — `historical._AssemblyTally`, frozen as `historical.AssemblyCounts(settled, rejected, duplicates)` with `kept` derived — never a second walk; walk B counts nothing. For that the phases hand their DAY SLICES back UNFILTERED (the read-back `keep` saved no memory — a walk holds one record at a time either way — and hid every rejection from any count); the three filters that must drop records before they are held — the live frontier's sink, and each sequential fallback — report what they dropped (`note_rejected`; the frontier as its window's emitted count minus the spool's length, only once the windowed path has succeeded). `_assembled_records` tests the prefilter BEFORE the window (pure filters, so nothing yielded changes) so a walk that counts nothing still skips the settlement parse for every rejected record. Counting therefore MOVES cost between records rather than only adding it: walk A must parse a rejected record's settlement to know whether it lies in the window (a read-back-filtered slice never showed it that record), while a record the prefilter passes now pays one prefilter call per walk instead of two. Measured over the two walks together on real day-slice records streamed through `_DaySliceStream` (the first 150,000 of the 2026-09-17, -21 and -22 live slices, split by class, the 392 passing ones replicated under unique tickers; two runs): +0.15 to +0.46 µs per rejected record, −1.46 µs per passing record, and −0.12 to −0.18 µs/record (−1.5% to −2.3%) on a blend at 32.6% passing, with break-even at 10–24% passing. 32.6% is about the review window's past-day pass rate — at most 7,274,215 assembled of its 22,175,942 past-day records, at least the frontier note's 7,190,452 on 2026-09-22 alone — so there counting costs nothing measurable; a window where almost nothing passes (a head-of-slice sample passes 0.09%) pays at most the rejected-class figure, about 10 s at 22,175,942 records against that window's 2,382 s fresh fetch. The lines now read `Historical endpoint: N eligible markets of M records settled in the window before the archive cutoff (R rejected by the prefilter <tag>, D duplicate or blank tickers)`, `Live endpoint: N eligible markets of M recently settled records in the window (…)` and `Assembled N eligible markets of M records settled since <start> (…)` (`settled markets` in place of `eligible markets` and no rejected clause without a prefilter). The total is stamped into the assembled cache as the informational meta key `assembly_counts` and read back into `CorpusProvenance.assembly_counts` — `None` for every legacy cache and every streamed one written before it (the 2026-09-17 cache on disk), and dropped with a WARNING on a hit whose counts do not keep exactly the records its validating walk counted. The backtester's two lines are `backtester._log_corpus_prefilter`: with provenance (a fetched corpus) `Markets to analyze: N eligible — the eligibility prefilter (<tag>) ran during assembly and rejected R of the S records settled in the window (…)` (or that the cache records no count), then `Eligibility prefilter re-check: 0 of N markets rejected — none expected, …` — and a re-check that rejects anything on such a corpus is a WARNING, since only a `_can_ever_enter` change without a `SETTLED_PREFILTER_CACHE_TAG` bump, or an altered cache, can make it (the first line then reads `Markets to analyze: N assembled as eligible, E still eligible after the re-check below — …`, so it never calls all N eligible above a WARNING that says some are not); without provenance (a plain list, i.e. a test stub) the old `Eligibility prefilter: skipping X/N …` line stands, since that corpus's assembly is unknown. The counts are RECORDS, not markets: a repeat the prefilter passes is a `duplicate`, but a repeat it rejects is counted in `rejected` once per arrival — telling them apart would mean holding every rejected ticker, the residency SS-1 removed. The dashboard's census wording (`N of M eligible markets`) was already right and is unchanged; the page does not render the assembly counts. Pinned by `tests/test_historical.py::TestAssemblyCounts`, `::TestStreamedAssemblyParity` (hand-worked counts on the old-merge fixture), `_assert_counts_cover_the_prefilter` inside `TestShardedFetch::test_prefilter_assembly_equals_postfilter`, `TestSequentialFallbacksApplyKeep::test_prefilter_through_the_fallbacks_equals_postfilter` and `::test_a_frontier_that_rejects_records_is_counted_once_on_every_path` (the settled count is the same with and without a prefilter on the sharded/windowed path and on every fallback; the first two run at the real date, whose frontier day holds no fixture record, so the third pins "now" inside the fixture, making the frontier's own sink reject records, and forces the live fallback to discard that frontier only after it has spooled — a frontier counted anyway reads 14 settled records instead of 11), and `tests/test_backtester.py::TestPrefilterLinesSayItRanDuringAssembly`. The fetch's per-100-page progress lines (`N markets kept so far`) are unchanged and still count what the fetch itself kept (binary results in its window) BEFORE the prefilter: they measure fetch progress, not eligible markets, and renaming them is an open follow-up. (A market that passes it but shares neither grouping key with another eligible market is not grouped either — since SS-1 `_prepare_candidates` never copies it into the list it groups, and since SS-1's Commit C the fetch streams the corpus off disk, so it is resident only as the record in hand — except on a hit on a LEGACY `.json` cache, which is still one list resident until the second walk ends; see TS-07.)

**The backtest's entry checkpoint IS the scheduler's run instant (2026-09-27).** The live bot runs at `config.SCHEDULED_RUN` — Monday 09:00 America/Los_Angeles, i.e. 16:00 UTC under daylight time and 17:00 UTC under standard time — and the backtest used to open every trade at Monday 09:00 UTC, 7–8 hours before the live bot sees the book. Two read-only replays on 2026-09-27 over the cached 365-day corpus (start 2025-09-24, ladders on, band 0–0.5, tier floors off, k 0.80, cap 0.20) put the cost at +78.4% return / −9.3% max drawdown at 09:00 UTC against +26.3% / −24.2% at 09:00 Los Angeles (+36.3% / −23.8% at a flat 16:00 UTC) — approximate, because they re-scanned a corpus the v2 prefilter had already cut for the 09:00 UTC checkpoint, so pairs built on markets opened on a Monday between 09:00 UTC and the later checkpoint were missing at the later anchors; an exact figure needs a replay over a corpus assembled under the v3 prefilter. **That replay was run the same day (the A/B measurement, 2026-09-27):** the 365-day window from 2025-09-24, ladders on, DR-75 in place, over ONE v3 corpus the branch's production run assembled (2026-09-28 01:56 UTC, archive cutoff 2026-07-29), both arms through the full pipeline — the old arm with `backtester.SCHEDULED_RUN` patched to Monday 09:00 UTC, so its prefilter re-check cut the corpus back to the v2 set. 09:00 UTC → 09:00 Los Angeles: 9,505,434 → 11,070,031 eligible markets, 3,122 → 5,898 time-series pairs, 400 → 442 no-band tier-on entries (706 → 778 with the tiers off). The old checkpoint's scan over the new arm's candidates entered exactly the old arm's set (406 of 406 tiers on, 712 of 712 off), so v2 was result-neutral and the difference is the checkpoint alone. Band 0–0.5, tier floors off, k 0.80, cap 20%: +58.9% / −13.2% max DD → +1.9% / −26.4% (−2.2% without its top event, H1 −30.6% / H2 +28.9%); the same band at k 0.75 and a 5% cap: +26.9% → −1.8%; the primary cell (0–1, tiers on, k 0.75, cap 20%): +116.4% / −83.4% → +136.3% / −63.6%; pooled empirical k̂ 0.891 → 0.958 (tiers off 0.884 → 0.927). At a 20% cap the share of time-series cells with a positive return rose from 20.3% to 37.0% (tiers on) and 38.5% to 53.0% (tiers off), but the Spearman rank correlation of those cells' returns between the two checkpoints is only 0.68 (tiers on) and 0.58 (tiers off) — 0.48–0.68 across the 5%, 20% and uncapped grids: which configuration looks best moves with the checkpoint. Re-run from the same entries on the simulator after DR-76 (time-series retries, one open trade per ladder), the live-toggles cell reads +67.3% / −29.8% max DD at 09:00 UTC against **−11.9%** / −25.5% at 09:00 Los Angeles (−22.6% without its top event). The new best cells (tiers off, band 0.25–1, k 0.65: +311.3%) still rest on one event (KXFISAEXTEND-26MAY; −18.4% without it, second half −14.1%). Harness and outputs: `.git/checkpoint-tz/` (`ckpt_measure_ab_build.py`, `ckpt_measure_ab_grid.py`, `ckpt_measure_ab_robust.py`, `ab_grid.out`, `ab_robust.out`, `r1/`). `backtester._checkpoint_datetime(d)` is now `SCHEDULED_RUN.instant(d)` — the one definition, shared with `scheduler.py`'s host-clock check — and `_monday_timestamps` and `_can_ever_enter` step to `SCHEDULED_RUN.weekday` (the function names keep "monday"). `_find_entry` and `_simulate_at_discount` are untouched. Six things are load-bearing. (1) **The backtest's dates stay UTC dates** — `start_date`, entry dates, `scan_end`, the horizon, the feasibility window and the equity curve — which is sound only while every checkpoint lies on its own date in UTC and its wall time occurs exactly once (no daylight-saving gap or fold). Monday 09:00 Los Angeles is sound on every Monday from 0001 to 9999. `_prepare_candidates` checks it with `ScheduledRun.date_problems(start_date, today + _SCHEDULE_CHECK_DAYS_AHEAD)` (ten years out, since a listed market can close years ahead; a backtester-private constant beside `_DAY_SECONDS`, the scheduler's `_HOST_CLOCK_CHECK_WEEKS` precedent; about 2 ms from 2020) right after it reads today and BEFORE the feasibility short-circuit and any fetch, and raises `ValueError` naming the schedule and its first three problem dates — or that its zone cannot be resolved — rather than returning the validation `None`: a schedule that shifts dates (Los Angeles 20:00, Tokyo 08:00) or sits on a clock change (Sunday 02:30 / 01:30 Los Angeles) is a configuration error. Pinned by `tests/test_backtester.py::TestScheduleProblemsAreRefused`. (2) **The prefilter floors the checkpoint onto the candle grid.** A market can have a candle at or before checkpoint `t` only if it opened before `floor_P(t)`, the start of the candle period (`CANDLESTICK_PERIOD_INTERVAL_MINUTES`, the epoch/UTC-day grid `_candle_window_open` floors onto) that holds `t` — from the measured rule that no candle ends at or before the start of the period a market opened in (see the prefilter gotcha). On the shipped schedule, on the hour, that is the old `open_time < checkpoint`; `backtester._checkpoint_floor(run, d)` computes it, `lru_cache`d on the pair (schedule, date) so a patched schedule never reads a stale entry, and returns `None` when the instant falls outside `datetime`'s range — that checkpoint then counts as reachable on the opening date and the close-date bound alone decides, so `_can_ever_enter` still never raises. The candle period is an input to the predicate that the tag does not name, so changing `CANDLESTICK_PERIOD_INTERVAL_MINUTES` needs a `SETTLED_PREFILTER_CACHE_TAG` bump (the constants rule). The predicate stays exactly the scan's reachable set for any sound schedule (`TestCanEverEnterMatchesTheScan`, parametrized over five schedules including 03:30 UTC and 00:00 UTC ones, on a grid spanning both clock changes), and costs 0.75 µs a call over 300,000 synthetic cache-shaped records (0.97–0.98 µs on Monday openings, 0.71–0.72 µs on the rest) against 0.76 µs for the Monday 09:00 UTC predicate in the same run. (3) **The cache tag is built when it is used** (`backtester._prefilter_cache_tag()`: `checkpoint-v3` plus the schedule's slug — see the cache-semantics gotcha, which also lists the v2 caches this orphans). On the shipped schedule v3 admits a SUPERSET of v2: the checkpoint is later on the same UTC date, so markets opened on a Monday between 09:00 UTC and 16:00/17:00 UTC are now kept (daily contracts opened after 09:00 UTC and closing before the next Monday among them). (4) **Never move the scheduler to `schedule`'s `.at(…, tz)`** to "make it match": that path goes through pytz, whose America/Los_Angeles table has no daylight time after 2037, so from 2038 it would fire at 10:00 PDT all summer. The scheduler stays on the host's clock and checks at startup that the host's clock lands its fires on `SCHEDULED_RUN.instant(d)` (`scheduler._host_clock_realises_run`); on a host that fails that check the CRITICAL says the backtest no longer replays its runs. (5) **Same-day cash release, pre-existing and unchanged.** `_simulate_at_discount` releases a trade's receipts on its exit DATE, whatever the hour, and sizes a checkpoint's entries on that date's opening portfolio value, spending only its cash (since 2026-09-29; before, on its opening cash) — so a receipt settling later on the entry date funds that morning's entries. At 09:00 UTC that window was up to 15 hours after the checkpoint; at 16:00/17:00 UTC it is 7–8 hours. (6) **Not comparable:** every backtest result, dashboard and assembled cache from before this change was entered at 09:00 UTC; entries, trades, returns, drawdowns and every eligible-market count move. Patch `backtester.SCHEDULED_RUN` in a test, never `config.*` (the module binds it by value); the tag and the floor cache follow the patch. The test fixtures' 09:00 UTC candle stamps (`tests/test_backtester.py`'s `_MONDAY_TS`, `_MONDAY2_TS` and `TestActiveTickerRelease._M2`) are read at or before the later checkpoint and were not moved: they are candle stamps, not checkpoints, so a fixture meaning "at the checkpoint" must build it from `backtester._checkpoint_datetime`. **The run records which checkpoint it entered at.** `run_backtest_sweep` logs `Entry checkpoint (backtest): Monday 09:00 America/Los_Angeles (config.SCHEDULED_RUN, the live scheduler's run time): 16:00/17:00 UTC in this window` right after the size-cap line, before any fetch: the distinct UTC times of `_monday_timestamps(start_date, today)` (`backtester._checkpoint_utc_times`), `no checkpoint in this window` when there is none, and `UTC times not computable (<exception class name>)` (e.g. `(ZoneInfoNotFoundError)`) when they cannot be computed — the line never raises, so `_prepare_candidates` still refuses an unresolvable zone or an unsound schedule with its `ValueError`, and a `start_date` whose next run weekday lies past `date.max` still raises `OverflowError` there (pre-existing, from its own `_monday_timestamps` call). `BacktestSweep.entry_checkpoint` carries `SCHEDULED_RUN.label()` from both production constructions (the infeasible branch included), and `dashboard._entry_checkpoint_html` prints `Entry checkpoint: <label> — the live scheduler's run time` (`not recorded` for anything but a non-empty string) on its own header line directly under the run-settings line; `_run_settings_html` is untouched and the header is outside `TestGoldenSections`' digests. Pinned by `tests/test_backtester.py::TestEntryCheckpointLogging`, `::TestRunBacktestSweep::test_feasibility_short_circuit_records_the_entry_checkpoint` and `::test_the_sweep_records_the_schedule_it_entered_at`, `::TestBandSweep::test_the_sweep_records_its_entry_checkpoint`, and `tests/test_dashboard.py::TestEntryCheckpointHeader`.

**Time-series pair extraction is close-time-windowed, not naive O(n²).** For string-keyed (time-series) groups, `_extract_pairs()` sorts members by `close_time` and sweeps a nested loop with an early `break` once the window closes, bounded by `MAX_DEADLINE_GAP_DAYS + 1` days of margin — the outcome is O(n·window), not the naive O(n²), but it isn't a classic two-pointer sweep since the inner loop restarts from each outer index rather than advancing a shared second pointer. The window exists because `_find_entry()` unconditionally rejects any time-series pair whose close dates differ by more than `MAX_DEADLINE_GAP_DAYS`, so pairs outside it are skipped without ever being materialized as a candidate (counted since M10, never enumerated). The `+1` day is slack only (it can only add harmless boundary candidates that `_find_entry`'s exact cutoff then rejects, never drop a legitimate one). Same-title (3-tuple-keyed) groups have no deadline-gap concept and stay naive — the eligibility prefilter above is what keeps those groups small. Both branches also drop two events of one series (`_same_series_dicts`, plus `_identical_wording_dicts` on the time-series branch — see the one-series gotcha); grouping is deliberately left alone, so the rule lives in exactly one place on this path, as it does on the live one. The same-title branch additionally refuses, per visited candidate, a pair whose closes cannot be compared or sit more than `SAME_TITLE_MAX_CLOSE_GAP_SECONDS` apart (DR-74 — `_comparable_closes_dicts`, then `scanner.closes_apart`; see the close-gate gotcha). That is a pairing rule, not a window: the branch stays naive, and its groups are not close-filtered, so a member whose `close_time` cannot be parsed stays in its group and is refused per candidate on its own line. Since M10 each of those drops, and each same-event skip, is counted on its own silent-at-zero INFO line, and so are the two things the sweep sets aside before visiting anything — members without a readable `close_time`, and the pairs its window excludes (counted from the index at the window's `break`, once per outer member, so the sweep's cost is unchanged) — see the DR-67 observability paragraph. `config.LARGE_GROUP_WARN_THRESHOLD` (1000) logs a warning (not a cap) whenever a post-filter group is still large, as a canary that the prefilter/windowing are working as expected.

**A pair enters on its EARLIEST qualifying Monday that passes the Kelly gate (DR-75, 2026-09).** **The defect.** The backtest entered each pair on its FIRST qualifying Monday or never: `backtester._find_entry` — which applies only the price, deadline and band filters and holds no probability model, which is what lets one entry pass per band serve every `k` and every size cap — returned at the first Monday that passed them, and `_simulate_at_discount`'s Kelly gate, which runs later and per `k`, DROPPED a pair whose first Monday failed it and never looked at it again, even on a later Monday where it would have passed, while the weekly live run re-scans and re-sizes every Monday. That is what made "tier floors off" produce FEWER trades than "on": at band 0–1 and `k` = 0.75 in the 2026-09-26 20:45 run (365 days from 2025-09-24), 61 trades with the tiers on and 56 with them off — with the floors off, pairs first qualify weeks earlier at a thin spread on a wide book, Kelly refuses that price, and 13 groups lost their only trade. **Every qualifying Monday is recorded.** `_find_entry` now scans to `scan_end` and returns the first qualifying Monday's dict — the same eight keys, with the same values — plus `"later"`: a tuple of same-shaped dicts, one per LATER qualifying Monday, earliest first, `()` when the pair qualified on one Monday only. Each later dict carries that Monday's own date and four quotes, the pair's `gap_days`, and its own `mA`/`mB`: a same-title pair's canonical A is re-decided every Monday (the pricier side that week), so a later Monday can hold the legs the other way round — `tests/test_backtester.py::TestActiveTickerRelease`'s TX/TY pair has A = TX on 2026-01-05 and A = TY from 2026-01-12 — while a time-series pair's order never changes. They reference the SAME market dicts the first Monday does, never copies. `backtester._entry_mondays(entry)` — `(entry, *entry.get("later", ()))` — is the one reader, and its `.get` default is load-bearing: an entry built without the key (several tests build eight-key entries by hand) reads as a pair that qualified on one Monday, which is how it was simulated before the key existed. `tests/test_strategy.py::TestTimeSeriesKellyParity::test_ast_later_mondays_have_one_reader` lets the key be spelled — as a string, or as a keyword argument such as `dict(entry, later=...)` — only in `_find_entry`, `_entry_mondays` and the truncating writer `_split_halves`, anywhere in the package (so an unrelated `"later"` in any module fails it too; widen its allowed homes deliberately if one is ever needed — a spelling built at run time is out of its scope), and pins that `_log_qualifying_mondays`, `_simulate_at_discount`, `_ex_top_event` and `CapSweep.entry_events` read the Mondays through `_entry_mondays` while `_interval_calibration` and `_split_date` never call it. `tests/test_backtester.py::TestFindEntryRecordsEveryQualifyingMonday` checks the recorded Mondays against `_find_entry` itself restarted the day after each hit — on the golden pairs, TX/TY and 300 seeded random pairs whose Mondays are refused between qualifying ones by every per-Monday filter — which is independent of how one pass COLLECTS the Mondays, but not of the per-Monday filters and the candle lookup both sides share; and it refuses Monday 2 of three by each filter in turn (the band ceiling, the tier, the leg-price-sum ceiling, a dead YES quote, an unparseable quote, the fee, a non-positive spread, a dead NO leg), each a case a scan that ENDED at the first refusal after a hit would fail. The independent anchors are `TestPrepareEntriesGolden`'s rows, which now carry each pair's later dates, computed by restarting the PRE-change code, and the critics' cross-version checks: the pre-change code, restarted, agreed with the new `"later"` on all 7,940 later Mondays of four seeded random corpora and on 54,132 real calls (each entered pair, both ways round) across the 365-day window's 54 band passes (measured 2026-09-27). `_find_entry` still returns checkpoint prices only — every qualifying Monday's now, never a daily series; the day-end prices that value an open trade at market are sampled separately, from the same candles, by `_attach_leg_quotes` (see the market-valuation paragraph after DR-61's). **Each leg's candles are read in ONE forward pass** (`backtester._candles_at_or_before`): scanning to `scan_end` made a per-Monday `_candle_at_or_before` — still the reference rule, unchanged — rescan each leg's list from its first candle at every Monday, O(Mondays × candles); the pass returns exactly that function's answer at each Monday, for any candle order, in O(candles + Mondays), both pinned by `tests/test_backtester.py::TestCandlesAtOrBefore`. Measured 2026-09-27 against the code before DR-75 and against a per-Monday `_candle_at_or_before` lookup: the 54 band passes over the 365-day window's (from 2025-09-24) entered pairs take 3.77 s, against 5.15 s and 6.25 s (best of 3 runs each); Phase 1 on the DR-73 calibration corpus (10,733 pairs, start 2020-01-01, ladders on; both tier settings' no-band pre-pass and rescans) 5.77 s, against 6.54 s and 7.42 s (best of 2); and a synthetic pair qualifying on all 52 Mondays of its one-year window, over two years of hourly candles per leg, 1.56 ms a pass, against 0.57 ms (which recorded its first Monday only) and 39.66 ms. Reading on past the first qualifying Monday (every Monday of the window is looked up, those `max_horizon_days` then skips included) means a malformed candle there — a missing price key (`KeyError`), or a `"ts"` missing or not comparable with an int (`KeyError`, `TypeError`) — now raises where the first-hit scan left it unread, the failure a pair that never qualified always had; the candle cache holds none (0 malformed of 4,629,341 candles in 99,001 cached files, scanned 2026-09-27). Each entry pass logs one INFO line, zero included (DR-66): `Qualifying Mondays at band <floor-ceiling>[ with the tier floors off]: N over M entries (P qualify on more than one)`, from `_prepare_entries` right after its `Prepared …` line and from `_sweep_from_candidates` right after each band's tier-on and tier-off `Prepared …` line (36 + 18 lines on a default CLI run); it shares no prefix with the lines tests filter on. **Retention**, supplementing the size-cap paragraph's "one small entry dict per band per entry": each later Monday adds one small dict per band per entry, referencing the pair's own two market dicts, so no market dict is added. On the 365-day window from 2025-09-24 (measured 2026-09-27) that is, per band, 272 at the no-band band (677 qualifying Mondays over 405 entries; 133 pairs qualify on more than one, at most 11) and at most as many at any other single band, whose qualifying Mondays are a subset of the no-band band's pair by pair, and 812 at the tier-off no-band band (1,523 over 711; 322 on more than one, at most 20) and at most as many at any other single binding band; over the whole sweep, 4,930 across the 36 tier-on bands and 6,778 across the 18 tier-off ones, about +3.9 MB traced. Their quotes are the candles' own float objects, shared by every band's dict for one Monday, so once the candles are released they keep alive only 1,434 floats (34 KB) that no first-Monday entry already holds. **The Kelly gate walks them.** `_simulate_at_discount`'s Pass 1b computes the same leg prices, fee, `profit_ratio_entry`, `kelly_b_entry` (DR-62's fee-in-the-denominator arithmetic, unchanged), `p`, `q` and `kelly_f` for each of a pair's qualifying Mondays in date order (`for monday in _entry_mondays(entry)`, the `time_series_profit_prob` call still inline there, as `test_ast_backtester_prices_through_helper` requires) and stops at the first positive fraction; a pair none of whose Mondays passes is dropped as before. Everything after the gate reads the CHOSEN Monday — its quotes, its date and its legs, which for a same-title pair can be the other way round from its first Monday's — so the trade's `entry_date`, `entry_pA`..`entry_nB`, `ticker_a`/`ticker_b` and every other per-leg field, `event_ticker`, `holding_days`, the ranking key `entry_monthly_ratio` and the orientation `_settlement_receipt` pays on are all that Monday's; only `gap_days` is read off the entry itself, since it is the same on every Monday. The H1 records `_split_halves` rebuilds (below) are transient — a `HalfSplit` keeps numbers only — so the gate adds no retention. The earliest passing Monday wins even over a later one with a larger fraction (the live run would have entered there), and `SweepPoint.peak_kelly_fraction` is the CHOSEN Monday's fraction, never the largest over a pair's Mondays. The choice reads `k` and nothing the cap or the cash touches, so the cap still never moves which candidates pass, which Monday each enters on or the peak — `CapSweep`'s reuse rule stands. Every recorded Monday has already passed the fee check (`net_spread > 0`), so `kelly_b > 0` on each and the gate fails only on `p − q/b ≤ 0` (or skips a time-series Monday whose quote is crossed on either market, DR-78); a same-title pair (fixed `p`) can be rescued too, independently of `k`, so `same_title_point` stays valid simulated once. At a given `k` the premise-violation count can only RISE against the first-Monday rule: every pair that reached the premise check before still does (its first Monday still passes first), and a rescued pair now reaches it too — a time-series pair's legs, and so its settlement cell, are the same on every Monday. **What stays on the FIRST Monday, deliberately:** the interval calibration — read at the Monday a simulation traded on, that Monday, and whether the pair trades at all, would depend on `k`, each `k` would produce its own `k̂`, and the pairs a `k` rejects on every Monday would leave the sample: the assumption would shape the measurement meant to test it, the circularity `_interval_calibration` already refuses, so it stays one number per band, unchanged by DR-75; the split date — every band × `k` cell must split at one date or their halves cannot be compared, and the traded Monday moves with `k` while the first does not; and the populations and the band sweep's pre-pass, which read only which pair it is. **The split-half check.** Each pair stays in the half its FIRST qualifying Monday puts it in, so both halves' entry counts are what they were, but `_split_halves` gives an H1 pair only its Mondays BEFORE the split — otherwise the gate could enter an H1 pair on a second-period Monday inside the first-period simulation. It rebuilds the record (`{**rec, "entry": {**entry, "later": kept}}`) only when a Monday is cut and never assigns `entry["later"]` in place: entry records are shared by every population, band and CapSweep holding them (the same-title ones at every band and under both tier settings). An H1 pair is NOT re-listed in H2: each half's candidates must stay a subset of the full run's, each on the Monday the full run chose or absent, or a half could reach a Kelly fraction above the full run's peak (re-listing a pair's post-split Mondays would let H2 enter it on one the full run did not choose) and `CapSweep`'s shared copies of the halves, which build them through the same function, would stop being exact. The residual: an H1 pair whose only Kelly-passing Mondays fall on or after the split trades in neither half, though the full run trades it. **Events on any Monday.** `_ex_top_event` drops every entry whose market-A event ticker is the top event on ANY of its qualifying Mondays, and `CapSweep.entry_events` lists market A's event on every Monday, because a trade records the chosen Monday's market A and a same-title pair's canonical A is decided per Monday; the dashboard reaches entries only through `entry_events()`, so `dashboard.py` did not change. Both are deliberately WIDER than the trades. `_ex_top_event` also drops a flipping same-title entry whose CHOSEN Monday at the point's `k` names another event, taking a trade filed under that other event out of the re-simulation (a hand-built repro: +0.0000% where dropping only the entries whose chosen Monday names the top event gives +7.3407%). That can move the ex-top return either way — down when the extra trade won, as there, up when it lost (the same repro with that trade losing: +0.0000% against −19.9993%). It is latent on the real corpus — none of the 365-day window's same-title entries qualifies on a second Monday (tiers on and off, checked 2026-09-27), and a time-series pair's A never changes — and it leaves `CapSweep`'s reuse rule intact: the re-simulated entries are still a subset of the point's, each entering on the Monday its own gate picks at that `k`, so their peak is no higher. `entry_events()` is a SUPERSET of the events any cell's trades carry: it lists every entry, traded or not (the golden fixture's voided TA/TB and WA/WB pairs never trade — a voided pair cannot — yet their events SNOWA and HAILA are listed), and a flipping entry's event on every Monday. **Measured by the prototype this change was specified from** (2026-09-27, before any code change: the real 365-day corpus's entries re-prepared from the cache, every later Monday enumerated by re-running the pre-DR-75 `_find_entry` from the day after each hit, and replayed through the real `_simulate_at_discount`; band 0–1, population "all", 20% cap, $10,000; trades / wins / return): at `k` = 0.75, tiers on 61/28/+111.8% → 61/30/+117.1% and tiers off 56/32/+131.0% → 79/49/+109.4%. With DR-75 the tiers off trade at least as often as the tiers on at every `k` — more up to 0.85, the same from 0.90, where the two CONVERGE (44/18/−19.8% at 0.90, 27/6/−27.6% at 0.95 either way) — and returns are NOT uniformly better (tiers off at 0.85 fall from +55.9% to +11.2%): a fidelity fix, not a profit fix. Nor are the tiers-on GAINS at 0.65–0.75 (+82.8% → +112.9%, +96.1% → +121.2%, +111.8% → +117.1%) fidelity: they come from one look-ahead swap in one group, without which DR-75 matches or trails the first-Monday rule at all three (see the Residuals). **Residuals.** A pair that passes the gate on a Monday but that Pass 2 cannot take there — no cash, not one contract affordable, a ticker still in an open trade, exact fees eating the profit — is NOT retried on a later Monday, where the live bot would retry it the next week (0–10 pairs per scenario on the 2026-09-26 run's band 0–1, 1 at `k` = 0.75 with the tiers on); that needs a week-by-week Pass 2, a separate change. The one-pair-per-group choice still happens in Pass 1, before any cash is considered, and it is LOOK-AHEAD, as it was before DR-75: `best_by_group` keeps the largest `entry_monthly_ratio` among candidates dated on DIFFERENT Mondays, so its winner can be dated after a group-mate whose Kelly fraction was already positive on an earlier Monday (the one a live run would have traded then), and that ratio's horizon runs to the legs' `close_time`, which for a settled market is its REALIZED close — before the scheduled one for any market that resolved early (the comment above `expected_days` in `_simulate_at_discount`, which used to claim the metric read only what was known at entry, now says so). Measured 2026-09-27 on the 365-day window from 2025-09-24 (band 0–1, 20% cap; the adversary critic's harness, re-run), the groups whose winner is dated after a Kelly-passing group-mate: already 20 of the 67 with a candidate at every `k` ≤ 0.55 under the first-Monday rule with the tiers on; at `k` = 0.75, 16 of 62 → 19 of 62 under DR-75 with the tiers on, and 10 of 56 → 21 of 81 with them off. DR-75 adds cases because a pair rescued onto a later Monday can now displace a group-mate that passed on an earlier one, and one such case carries the headline numbers — the YouTube TV/Disney ladder. At `k` = 0.75, NOV07/NOV30 passes on 2025-11-03 (Kelly +0.0989) and the first-Monday rule trades it (−$776.85); NOV17/NOV30 fails that Monday (−0.197), passes on 11-10 (+0.109) and wins the group on its ratio, 1.844 against 1.686, only because NOV30's realized close is 2025-11-15 (to the stated Nov 30 deadline the two ratios are 0.461 and 0.749); DR-75 trades it (+$252.82). With the tiers on (band 0–1, population "all"), the first-Monday rule / DR-75 / DR-75 without that one rescue return +82.76% / +112.93% / +82.76% at `k` 0.65, +96.14% / +121.16% / +95.00% at 0.70 and +111.81% / +117.08% / +97.85% at 0.75 — **so the tiers-on gains in the prototype's table are this one look-ahead swap, not a fidelity improvement**: without it DR-75 matches or trails the first-Monday rule at all three. The follow-up — an operator decision, NOT part of DR-75 — is to make the contest causal (per group, the earliest-dated Kelly-passing candidate, ties broken by ratio) and/or to measure the horizon to the stated deadline; either would also move figures that predate DR-75. The live bot is untouched (`strategy.py` never imports `backtester`). Pinned by `tests/test_backtester.py::TestKellyPicksTheEarliestPassingMonday`, `::TestSplitHalvesKeepEachHalfOnItsSide`, `::TestExTopEventReadsEveryMonday`, `::TestCapSweepEntryEventsReadEveryMonday`, `::TestCalibrationReadsTheFirstMonday`, `::TestLaterMondayThroughTheCapSweep` and `::TestTierOffSweep::test_at_k_0_90_the_ladder_enters_on_its_first_kelly_passing_monday` (the golden tier-off ladder first qualifies on 2026-01-05 at a Kelly fraction of −0.016 at `k` = 0.90 and now enters on 2026-01-12 there, at +0.032). Checked 2026-09-27 against independent emulations of the rule on the pre-change code (built separately by the implementer and by a reviewer) (each pair handed to the old simulator on its earliest Kelly-passing Monday, H1 cut at the split, events read on every Monday): identical on every point, size-cap cell, check and log line of seven golden-fixture runs, and on all 216 simulations and checks of four seeded random corpora (1,803 entries). Against the old code itself, the golden fixture moved only at `k` = 0.90 with the tier floors off (the ladder: 54 tier-off points and their 1,080 cap cells), and every one of the 156 random-corpus differences traced to a re-dated pair or the any-Monday event rule. **Every backtest trade count, return and drawdown recorded before DR-75 was measured under the first-Monday rule**, and a figure is unaffected exactly when no pair it covers has a later qualifying Monday that changes whether, when or on which legs it enters at the `k` it quotes (or, for an excluding-top-event figure, puts the dropped event on market A). Two examples that DO move, both time-series: the 365-day ladder figures in `config.TIME_SERIES_SAME_EVENT_LADDERS`' comment (+111.8%, and −92.8% without the top event — re-simulated from the prototype's entries, +111.81% and −92.79% under the first-Monday rule, +117.08% and −90.05% under DR-75) and the tier-off paragraph's "47 of those 278 clear the Kelly gate", which counts the gate at each pair's first-Monday prices (under DR-75 a pair clears it when any of its qualifying Mondays does, so 47 is only a lower bound). The DR-74 table's shipped-rule row (close within 1 h: 6 entries, 3 trades, +4.8%, −1.9%) is unaffected: that table replays same-title candidates only, and none of the six same-title entries of that 365-day window qualifies on a second Monday (the prototype's entries, tiers on and off, checked 2026-09-27); its other rows replay same-title rules the repo no longer runs and were not re-measured. **This paragraph amends, without editing them,** the interval-discount paragraph's `k`-dependent tail — its Kelly gate now walks each pair's qualifying Mondays — and its split-date sentences: the split date is each pair's FIRST qualifying Monday, and an H1 pair's later Mondays are cut at it. Both old sentences remain true as written. **One interaction:** the dashboard's time-series spread calibration (#71) reads each traded pair's entry spread — its entry mid spread since DR-78 (`_entry_mid_spread`), `pB − pA` before it — which is now the spread on the Monday the Kelly gate chose — `k`-dependent — rather than the pair's first qualifying Monday's. **DR-76 amends this paragraph, without editing it** (see its backtest paragraph): for TIME-SERIES pairs the Kelly gate no longer stops at the first passing Monday — every passing Monday is a candidate, and Pass 2 trades the pair on the first of them it can take — so the residual "a pair Pass 2 cannot take is NOT retried" is resolved for time-series pairs, and their contest is no longer the whole-window `best_by_group` look-ahead but causal across Mondays: the one-open-trade-per-ladder rule decides, Monday by Monday, with each Monday's candidates ordered by ratio. `SweepPoint.peak_kelly_fraction` is the largest `f*` over every Monday a pair may be traded on (every passing Monday of a time-series pair, a same-title pair's first), no longer the chosen Monday's, because a time-series pair can be traded on any of them. Which of its passing Mondays a time-series pair trades on now depends on the cap, the cash and the open ladders as well as `k` — a different cap leaves different cash on an earlier Monday — so this paragraph's "the cap still never moves which candidates pass, which Monday each enters on or the peak" no longer holds for the Monday a time-series pair enters on; the cap still moves neither which Mondays pass the gate nor the peak, and `CapSweep`'s reuse rule stands because the peak covers every Monday a pair may be traded on. Its "One interaction" moves the same way: a traded time-series pair's entry spread (#71; its entry mid spread since DR-78) is the spread on the Monday it was taken, which depends on the cap, the cash and the open ladders as well as `k`. `_split_halves` still never re-lists an H1 pair in H2, for the same subset reason, which now matters for same-title pairs only: the full run tries a same-title pair only on its first passing Monday, so a re-listed copy could enter H2 on a Monday the full run never tries (a re-listed time-series pair's Mondays would all be full-run candidates). Same-title pairs keep this paragraph's rules unchanged (with `add_to_held` off): first passing Monday, the whole-window group contest, no retry. **The portfolio-value change of 2026-09-29 amends the residual's cash causes too**: Pass 2 now shrinks a trade to the cash left (see the portfolio-value paragraph under Key Patterns), so "no cash" and "not one contract affordable" are one cause — a budget (the Kelly share of the portfolio value, never more than the cash left) that buys less than one contract pair — and a same-title pair with cash for fewer contract pairs than Kelly asked for trades at that smaller size rather than being skipped.

**`secrets.json` may be missing outer braces.** `auth.py` wraps it if needed. New code must call `auth.build_client()` rather than reading `secrets.json` directly.

**`CandidatePair.max_contracts = 0` means NOT ENRICHED, and nothing else.** Before `enrich_with_orderbook_prices()` it is the dataclass default (`depth_levels` is `()` alongside it) and `strategy.compute_trade` reads it as "no depth cap", taking the single-shot sizing path. After enrichment it is the affordability-bounded qualifying depth — **how many contracts the pair's written leg prices are valid for** — and enrichment never writes 0: a pair whose book holds under one contract at the gap, or whose budget cannot afford one contract pair, is dropped with `tradeable=False` and its own INFO line. That closes the older trap, where `max_contracts` came from `int(total_qty)` over a `float` quantity sum and a qualifying book with `0 < total_qty < 1` enriched to `max_contracts=0` with `tradeable=True`, which `compute_trade` then read as *uncapped*. A pair marked `tradeable=False` may still carry `max_contracts=0` from never having reached the writeback (unavailable orderbook, no qualifying depth, unaffordable) — check `pair.tradeable` first, as before.

**Backtest uses prod credentials**, not sandbox. The Kalshi historical API endpoint doesn't exist on the sandbox. `historical.py` always calls `build_prod_live_client()`.

**Dev mode reads no positions (`get_held_positions`)** because the sandbox account is separate and has no positions from production. Don't add held-ticker filtering to `_run_dev()`; for the same reason a dev run never adds to a held pair, whatever its `add_to_held_pairs` says (`main.py --help` says the flag is for production runs only), and has no positions-value check (`main._checked_positions_value` runs in production only). For the same reason it never calls `scanner.resolve_held_ladders` and hands the finder and `select_portfolio` no held ladders (DR-76); `select_portfolio`'s within-run rule — at most one time-series trade per ladder among the specs it picks — applies in dev as in prod, since it lives inside `select_portfolio`. Holding nothing, dev spends its virtual `--sandbox-balance` as both the portfolio value and the cash (`_run_dev` hands enrichment, `_compute_trade_specs` and `select_portfolio` the same number). Dev also never sells, whatever its `sell_at` says: it holds nothing, so `seller.plan_sales` is never called there (`main.py --help` says `--sell-at` is for production runs only).

**`normalize_title()` pattern order matters.** `_DATE_PATTERNS` in `scanner.py` applies patterns sequentially. Longer/more specific patterns must come before shorter ones to avoid partial matches leaving date fragments. If adding new patterns, prepend them before similar shorter ones.

**Event-title resolution is bounded, parallel, paced, and must stay loud.** `_load_or_build_event_titles` resolves `event_ticker` → title in three phases: bulk `get_events` listings (settled/closed/open), the MVE listing (capped at `MVE_TITLE_LOOKUP_MAX_PAGES`), then a per-ticker `/events/{ticker}` fallback. That fallback costs **one HTTP round trip per ticker** and was written for a handful of stragglers — but a 21-day window measured **289,235 unresolved tickers** live (2026-08-03), and the 2026-09-24 7-day run 3,987,139, where the original sequential, uncapped loop ground for hours emitting nothing, which is indistinguishable from a hang. It is now capped at `EVENT_TITLE_FALLBACK_MAX_LOOKUPS` (5000) and run across `EVENT_TITLE_FALLBACK_MAX_WORKERS` (8) threads, each pausing `EVENT_TITLE_FALLBACK_RATE_LIMIT_SLEEP_SECONDS` (0.15 s) after every lookup, success or failure — the `fetch_candlesticks` idiom (DR-51). Unpaced, 5,000 lookups drew 268 retried HTTP 429s in 2m03s; pacing lowers the rate but does not guarantee zero 429s — the candlestick fetch paced the same way ran at about 24 requests/s and still drew them on 4–5% of requests. **How the cap is spent (DR-51):** when every still-unresolved ticker fits under it, all are looked up (sorted); when they do not, the cap goes to NON-combo tickers only, in ticker order with the tickers the accumulator has never answered first (then its stored pills, then tickers it already titles — only the first group exists with the cache on), and every combo ticker (the KXMVE family, read through `scanner.event_series`) is deferred. A combo's event title has no measured effect on which pairs form — DR-54/55 refuse every combo-vs-combo same-title pair, and on the 2026-09-08/09 day slices no combo shared even the time-series key with a non-combo — while a non-combo's does (TS-11). The old single sorted list spent the whole cap on whatever sorted before "KXMVE" plus the head of the combo block, and on the 2026-09-24 run skipped `KXNFLEVERYWEEKCOMPETE-27` (34 markets) among the 3,982,139 tickers it skipped. This decides which LOOKUPS to spend, never an MVE exclusion: combo markets are still fetched, cached, grouped and paired by the same rules. **What it does change is an input to those rules.** A combo the old sorted cap reached was looked up and titled — that run's census logged `event_title` on 27,934 of 7,274,215 eligible records, 18,671 of them among its 18,705 non-combo records, so 9,263 combo records carried a title — and a deferred combo now stays blank unless the accumulator already holds its title. That moves those markets' grouping keys and the `event_title` and deadline-phrasing census figures, so a post-DR-51 fresh backtest's outcome-label census is not comparable with a pre-DR-51 one; it does not change which pairs form, because the 2026-09-08/09 measurement above was taken on day-slice records, which carry no `event_title` at all (it is patched in at assembly). **A deferred ticker is not looked up and nothing is stored for it (DR-51)** — its return value is whatever the accumulator already holds (`""` unless an earlier run titled it), and a later run tries it again. For non-combo tickers that later run advances past this one's slice either way: with the cache on because every ticker this call answered is no longer unresolved, and under `--no-cache` (which re-resolves every requested ticker) because the tickers the accumulator has never answered take the cap first — a plainly sorted cap looked up the same head on every `--no-cache` run and never reached the tail. Only a genuine answer is stored: a title, or the `""` poison pill for a lookup that failed or an event listed without a title, so a genuinely unresolvable ticker is not re-paid every run (no failed lookup appears in any retained backtest log). A deferral that includes non-combo tickers is a WARNING naming both counts; a combo-only deferral, expected on every bulk window and without pairing effect, is INFO, so the WARNING keeps meaning something. Every phase logs progress, and one closing INFO line partitions every ticker the CALL was asked about — resolved by this run (bulk listings / per-ticker lookups), answered from the accumulator, recorded as unresolvable, deferred — and gives the accumulator's size before and after (DR-42; the old line printed the whole seeded accumulator as "this run" — 7,986,570 on a run that resolved at most about 7,525 — and "answered from the accumulator" as 0 by construction whenever the cache was on). **Never make this phase silent again, and never remove the cap** without replacing it with something else bounded. The bulk listings are bounded too, by a productivity bail-out (`EVENT_TITLE_LISTING_MAX_BARREN_PAGES`, 50 consecutive pages resolving nothing → move on), the same idiom as `MVE_MAX_EMPTY_PAGES`. That matters because a listing is an O(all events) scan for a specific ticker set, and `get_events` **excludes MVE events by API design** — so for an MVE-heavy miss set it can never resolve them no matter how long it pages. Live-measured 2026-08-03: the `settled` listing resolved 8,696 of 289,235 tickers in its first ~500 pages, then 9 more across the next 400.

**MVE event-title plumbing.** The live scanner attaches an `_event_title` attribute to each `Market` object inside `fetch_open_events_with_markets()`. The backtest stores it as the `event_title` key in cached market dicts. Both pair-finder paths (`find_time_series_pairs`, `find_same_title_pairs`) and their backtester counterparts (`_group_by_normalized_title`, `_group_by_exact_title`) require this to be set for MVE markets — without it they fall back to grouping by market title (plus, on the time-series side, the outcome label) alone, which would let cross-event option-label collisions through. If you add a new entry point that produces markets, attach `_event_title` (or `event_title` in dict form) before passing into the grouping functions. Old backtest caches written before this change have no `event_title` field; re-run with `--no-cache` to refresh.
